at which the two classes meet is a function of the growth factor and the
reproduction primitives alone. Two things follow by substitution. No
instrument reaches the *level* of either index except through $g$; and
any perturbation preserving stationarity moves both levels by the same
proportion, $d\ln x_W = d\ln x_C = \gamma^{-1}\,d\ln g$. A class cannot
lower the other’s reproduction index without lowering its own in the
identical proportion. What the instruments can still move is the subject
of the rest of this section.

At the no-social-wage corner the index *is* the object of interest. With
$x_j = c_j$, index equalisation is per-capita equalisation and
$c_C/c_W \equiv 1$ at every policy. The instruments are not idle there —
they move the class *ratio*, and $\nu^\ast$ is an explicit function of
$(\tau,\phi,i)$ whose $\phi = 0$ case is Proposition 2’s closed form.
What they cannot move is the wedge.

An escape must therefore make the per-head ratio something other than
the index ratio, which under these hypotheses leaves three exits and no
others. One may deny that a stationary ratio means equal growth —
through an unbounded ratio, cross-class flows in the law of motion, or
no rest point at all; Section 7 closes the first and last for this
chassis within the constant-share class, leaving the middle one, which
is a change to the model rather than an outcome of it. One may deny that
the map is injective in the index, so that a set of inputs shares a
growth factor: a lumpy map, whose gap Lemma 1$'$ caps at the widest
flat, or a non-monotone one, which Lemma 1$'$ shows is not capped. Or
one may accept both and prise the object of interest apart from the
index. A map of several arguments belongs to the third exit, not the
second: it leaves Lemma 1 standing and moves what Lemma 1 is about.

This paper takes the third exit, and what separates it from a
class-specific technology is that the difference is endogenous. The map
here is common in the index — $F(u) = Bu^{\gamma}$, the same $B$ and the
same $\gamma$ on both sides — and that buys less than it appears to.
Expanded as a map of private consumption alone, the worker’s
reproduction is $\big[B\,z^{\gamma(1-\theta)}\big]\,
c_W^{\gamma\theta}$, differing from the capitalist’s $B\,c_C^{\gamma}$
in both level and exponent. In per-capita consumption the technologies
are therefore class-specific, the compensating-differential argument of
Section 1 applies, and its verdict stands: the private gap offsets
something and is not by itself a condition advantage. What that argument
does not reach is whether the thing being offset is a primitive. A
class-specific efficiency parameter is exogenous, so it pins the wedge
and no instrument touches it. Here the shifter $B\,z^{\gamma(1-\theta)}$
is a state the contested fiscal instrument builds, and the same
relabelling that makes the verdict apply is what makes the wedge move:
$\partial \ln\big[B z^{\gamma(1-\theta)}\big]/\partial\phi = \gamma(1-\theta)/\phi \neq 0$.

That the two arguments are genuinely two is not settled by Section 3.
The risk is not that $z$ is redundant in the distribution state — even a
redundant $z$ leaves policy moving the wedge through $c_W$ — but that
$z$ is a function of the worker’s own first argument, in which case the
composite is a one-argument map in disguise, the wedge is a fixed
function of $c_W$, and policy reaches it only through the level. That is
settled at the computed cells, on the stationary policy family rather
than in the transition: $(\ln c_W^\ast,\ \ln z^\ast)$ has rank two in
$(\tau,\phi,i)$, and there is an explicit instrument direction along
which $c_W^\ast$ is stationary to first order while $\ln z^\ast$ moves
at $0.40$ per unit, which no such function admits. Section 3’s gate
answers a different question: it licenses $z$ as a third state, and a
state can be irreducible in the dynamics and still be pinned to $c_W$ at
every stationary policy.

The stochastic version of the first exit does not deliver either. Making
stationarity distributional rather than pointwise does break $g_W=g_C$
period by period, but a stationary log class ratio has zero mean drift,
so with a common isoelastic map the geometric means of the indices
equalise instead, and the arithmetic per-head gap that then opens is
exactly the Jensen term in the two classes’ risk difference —
compensation for risk.

In this model the index is the Cobb–Douglas composite, so Lemma 1 reads
$$c_W^{\theta}\,(G/N_W)^{1-\theta} = c_C, \tag{1}$$ the composite
equalising where private consumption does not.

The displacement that follows does not need that form, and saying so
before assuming it costs four lines. What the Cobb–Douglas composite
buys is the *constancy* of the elasticity below; what carries the *sign*
is monotonicity alone.

**Lemma 1$''$ (displacement without a functional form).** *Let
$x_C = c_C$ and let the worker’s index be $x_W = h(c_W,z)$ for an $h$
nondecreasing in $c_W$ and strictly increasing in $z$, with $F$ strictly
increasing as in Lemma 1. At any interior stationary class ratio
$h(c_W,z) = c_C$; and along that locus, at a fixed equalised index, a
larger public stock strictly lowers the private claim — if $(c_1,z_1)$
and $(c_2,z_2)$ both lie on it and $z_1 < z_2$, then $c_1 > c_2$.*

*Proof.* Lemma 1 gives $x_W = x_C$, which is $h(c_W,z) = c_C$; that step
uses strict increase of $F$ and no property of $h$. Suppose $z_1 < z_2$
and, for contradiction, $c_1 \le c_2$. Monotonicity in the first
argument gives $h(c_1,z_1) \le h(c_2,z_1)$; strict monotonicity in the
second gives $h(c_2,z_1) < h(c_2,z_2)$. Hence $h(c_1,z_1) < h(c_2,z_2)$,
while both equal $c_C$ — a contradiction. Therefore $c_1 > c_2$.
$\blacksquare$

No differentiability, no concavity, no continuity and no functional form
is used, and neither $\gamma$ nor $B$ appears: the displacement is an
order property of the stationarity locus rather than a feature of the
technology chosen to represent it.

The *level* comparison between the two classes is likewise an order
property, and it rests on a different hypothesis rather than on more of
the same one.

**Lemma 1$'''$ (ordering, without a functional form).** *Let $x_C = c_C$
and let the worker’s index be $x_W = h(c_W,z)$, with $F$ strictly
increasing as in Lemma 1. Suppose $h$ is **idempotent** — $h(c,c)=c$ for
every $c>0$, as any weighted mean is — and strictly increasing in $z$.
Then at any interior stationary class ratio,*
$$c_C > c_W \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad z > c_W .$$ *If $h$ is in
addition strictly increasing in $c_W$, then also
$c_C > c_W \iff z > c_C$, and the three quantities order as
$c_W < c_C < z$.*

*Proof.* Lemma 1 gives $h(c_W,z) = c_C$. Idempotence writes
$c_W = h(c_W,c_W)$, so strict increase in the second argument gives
$h(c_W,z) > h(c_W,c_W)$ exactly when $z > c_W$; the left side is $c_C$
and the right is $c_W$, which is the displayed equivalence. For the
second claim, suppose $z > c_W$. Strict increase in the *first* argument
gives $h(c_W,z) < h(z,z) = z$, so $c_C < z$; with $c_C > c_W$ from the
first claim this is $c_W < c_C < z$. Conversely suppose $z > c_C$ and,
for contradiction, $c_C \le c_W$. The first claim then gives
$z \le c_W$, whence $h(c_W,z) \ge h(z,z) = z$, that is $c_C \ge z$ — a
contradiction. $\blacksquare$

**Lemmas 1$''$ and 1$'''$ are independent, and both differences are
occupied.** Lemma 1$'''$ uses strict increase in the second argument and
nothing whatever about the first; Lemma 1$''$ uses monotonicity in the
first and nothing about idempotence, so neither is a corollary of the
other. Under $h(c_W,z) = 2z - c_W$ — idempotent and strictly increasing
in $z$, but falling in the private claim — the ordering holds while the
displacement **reverses**: on the locus $c_C = 10$, raising $z$ from $6$
to $8$ raises $c_W$ from $2$ to $6$. Under $h(c_W,z) = c_W + z$ —
strictly increasing in both arguments but not idempotent — the
displacement holds while the ordering **fails** at $z = c_W$: at
$c_W = z = 5$ the index is $c_C = 10 > c_W$, while $z > c_W$ is false.
Nor is the second claim’s extra hypothesis decorative: $h(c_W,z) = z$ is
idempotent, nondecreasing in $c_W$ and strictly increasing in $z$, and
it makes $z$ and $c_C$ coincide identically, so $z > c_C$ never holds
and the sandwich collapses. Theorem 1 is the Cobb–Douglas case of
**both** lemmas, and its two halves divide between them: the ratio
$c_C/c_W = (z/c_W)^{1-\theta}$ exceeds one exactly when $z > c_W$, which
is Lemma 1$'''$; the constant elasticity along the equalised-index locus
is Lemma 1$''$’s displacement. What the functional form adds is those
closed forms — under a composite with a non-unit substitution elasticity
the same objects vary with the stock — and not either sign.

**Theorem 1 (stationary incidence).** *Let Assumptions 1–2 hold with
$\theta \in (0,1)$, and let $(x,\nu,z)$ be any interior stationary class
ratio — any state at which $\nu'=\nu$, whether or not it is an
equilibrium. Then the private per-capita gap is a closed form in the
stationary quantities,*
$$\frac{c_C}{c_W} = \Big(\frac{z}{c_W}\Big)^{1-\theta}
                  = \Big(\frac{z}{c_C}\Big)^{(1-\theta)/\theta}, \tag{2}$$
*and, along the equalised-index locus — that is, holding $c_C$ fixed —
the elasticity of private worker consumption with respect to public
provision is constant and negative:*
$$\frac{\partial \ln c_W}{\partial \ln z}\Big|_{c_W^{\theta}z^{1-\theta}\,=\,c_C}
  \;=\; -\frac{1-\theta}{\theta} \;<\; 0. \tag{3}$$ *On that locus the
two private claims are ordered by the public stock alone: the capitalist
consumes more privately than the worker if and only if public provision
per worker exceeds the worker’s own private claim — $c_C > c_W$ exactly
when $z > c_W$, equivalently exactly when $z > c_C$.*

*Proof.* Under Assumption 2 the reproduction indices of Definition 2 are
$x_W = c_W^{\theta}z^{1-\theta}$ and $x_C = c_C$, and the outer map
$F(u)=Bu^{\gamma}$ is strictly increasing because $\gamma>0$. Lemma 1
therefore applies and gives $x_W = x_C$, which is exactly equation (1),
$c_W^{\theta}z^{1-\theta} = c_C$. Dividing (1) by $c_W$ gives
$c_C/c_W = c_W^{\theta-1}z^{1-\theta} = (z/c_W)^{1-\theta}$, the first
equality in (2). For the second, solve (1) for $c_W$: since $\theta>0$,
$c_W = \big(c_C\,z^{\theta-1}\big)^{1/\theta}$, whence
$c_C/c_W = c_C^{\,1-1/\theta} z^{(1-\theta)/\theta} = (z/c_C)^{(1-\theta)/\theta}$,
using $1-1/\theta = -(1-\theta)/\theta$. This is (2). For the ordering,
$t\mapsto t^{1-\theta}$ is strictly increasing on $(0,\infty)$ and takes
the value one at $t=1$, because $1-\theta>0$; so the first equality in
(2) gives $c_C>c_W$ exactly when $z>c_W$. For the second comparison, (1)
turns $z>c_C$ into $z>c_W^{\theta}z^{1-\theta}$, that is
$z^{\theta}>c_W^{\theta}$, and $\theta>0$ makes that $z>c_W$ in turn.
Each bound on $\theta$ is consumed once, and the two differ in standing:
$\theta=1$ is admitted by Assumption 2 and excluded by this theorem, and
there the locus forces $c_C=c_W$; $\theta\to0$ lies outside Assumption 2
altogether, and there it forces $z=c_C$. Neither is a limiting case of
the ordering — each is a collapse of it.

For (3), take logarithms of (1):
$\theta \ln c_W + (1-\theta)\ln z = \ln c_C$. This is an affine relation
among $\ln c_W$, $\ln z$ and $\ln c_C$. Differentiating along it at
fixed $c_C$ gives
$\theta\, \mathrm{d}\ln c_W + (1-\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\ln z = 0$, so
$\mathrm{d}\ln c_W/\mathrm{d}\ln z = -(1-\theta)/\theta$, which is
negative because $\theta \in (0,1)$ and constant because $\theta$ is a
parameter. $\blacksquare$

The corner is the limit of the theorem rather than a case of it, the
hypothesis $\theta\in(0,1)$ excluding it: both expressions are
continuous in $\theta$ at $1$, and letting $\theta\to1$ sends (2) to
$c_C/c_W\equiv1$ at every policy and (3) to zero. That is Proposition
2(i) recovered from the interior formulas rather than proved a second
time.

The derivative in (3) is taken holding the equalised index $c_C$ fixed,
which is the only reading under which it is a statement about the locus
(1) rather than about a particular comparative static of the
equilibrium. It is a fact about the shape of the stationarity locus, not
a prediction of how $c_W$ responds when an instrument moves — an
instrument generally moves $c_C$ too, and the total derivative then
carries an extra term. Section 6 supplies the equilibrium comparative
statics.

Equations (2)–(3) are the paper’s core object, and four readings follow.

The reproduction boundary is not broken; a private-consumption gap opens
instead. The composite (1) equalises, so on the reproduction metric the
classes remain equally well reproduced and the equalisation boundary
continues to bind — by Lemma 1, and not by analogy with the
compensating-differential argument of Section 1. The distinction
matters, because that argument’s verdict is about a gap that merely
offsets a difference in reproductive efficiency, and there is no such
difference here: the maps are common. What offsets the private gap is
public provision, not efficiency, and whether that offset is itself a
compensating differential is the welfare question Section 6 takes up
rather than one already answered. Unlike at the corner, where the
private gap is identically one at every stationary structure, $c_C/c_W$
is here a function of policy — of the public-provision ratio and the
instruments that fund it. The contested instrument reaches a
distributional object it could not touch before; it does not, by (1)
alone, reach welfare condition.

Equation (3) says that a worker sustained by a larger social wage
reproduces equally on a smaller private claim: public provision crowds
out private reproduction consumption at the stationary ratio, and
raising the fiscal split $\phi$ strictly lowers $c_W^\ast$ — from
$0.964$ at $\phi=0.20$ to $0.720$ at $\phi=0.80$ at the reported cells.
This is the Speenhamland shape, a public supplement co-moving against
the private claim. The name is used for the shape and not for a verdict
on the English allowance system, whose standard account Block and Somers
(2003) contest from four decades of scholarship. Its welfare sign is not
settled here either: the social wage is funded by taxing capital, so a
lower private $c_W$ paired with capital-funded public provision may
register as capital financing labour’s reproduction rather than as
labour being squeezed. Ranking the two configurations would need a
social welfare function over the two dynastic sizes, and this paper
writes none; nor would writing one settle the matter, since the
provision is funded by taxing capital and any ranking must therefore
price that transfer on both sides rather than read the worker’s private
claim alone.

One margin (2)–(3) do not name is not a per-capita object at all.
Equation (1) forces the two reproduction indices together at any
stationary ratio, and (2)–(3) say what may still differ per head; what
may also differ is the relative number on each side. Theorem 2(i) makes
$\ln\nu$ a first-order process whose $\Theta$ is free of $\nu$, so a
stationary ratio satisfies $\ln\nu^\ast=\Theta^\ast/\gamma$ — a closed
form in the rest-point state, and hence in the instruments and the
primitives, carrying $\ln\big[(1-i)(1-\tau)\big]$ explicitly. Class
condition, read on the index the reproduction technology itself uses,
cannot differ at a stationary ratio. Relative class size can, and the
instruments are among the things that set it. Which way any one
instrument moves it is a general-equilibrium question this paper does
not answer: $\Theta^\ast$ carries the instruments directly and again
through $(x^\ast,z^\ast)$, and neither channel is signed here. What the
model does deliver is that the two margins equal reproduction leaves
open — the private-consumption gap and the relative size of the two
classes — are both objects a fiscal instrument reaches, and neither of
them is per-capita reproduction itself. Equations (2)–(3) hold at any
stationary interior policy. Which policies a class chooses — and
therefore the positive incidence, who funds the social wage and who
captures the displacement — is an equilibrium object and is deferred to
Section 6. Whether the displacement of the private wage claim makes
public provision something a capitalist would rationally support is a
comparison of his own value at a fixed tax and rate, not a welfare
ranking. Computation 1 settles it at the reported cells; it is not
proved on the whole domain.

# 6. Equilibrium

The stationary results above are equilibrium-free. What requires the
equilibrium in $(x,\nu,z)$ — with the worker choosing $(\tau,\phi)$ and
the capitalist $i$ — is the *positive* incidence Section 5 defers: who
funds the social wage, who captures the displacement, and at what
levels.

**Computation 1 (the equilibrium and the support corollary).** *At the
reported cells
($\alpha=0.33,\ \beta=0.96,\ \gamma=0.5,\ \sigma_W=\sigma_C=2,\ \delta=0.08,\ \delta_G=0.05,\
\theta=0.6,\ \bar\tau=0.8$) and at $\omega=0$, constant-share best
responses reach a fixed point at every one of nine runs — three starts
by three initialisations — all proper on both clauses of (A5), and every
one selects the corner $\phi^\ast = 1$: the worker devotes the entire
tax to public reproduction infrastructure and nothing to the direct
transfer. The other instruments are start-dependent, as a constant-share
concept requires: the three distinct profiles are
$(\tau^\ast,\phi^\ast,i^\ast) = (0.51,1,0.410)$, $(0.61,1,0.390)$ and
$(0.72,1,0.350)$, one per start, each reached from all three
initialisations. The corner is not start-dependent. Every computed path
lands on an interior stationary state at machine precision, where the
composite equalisation (1) and the wedge (2) hold to $10^{-16}$, the
private gap taking the values $c_C/c_W = 1.77,\ 1.96,\ 2.26$ at the
three constant-share equilibria. Across fourteen distinct cells of a
$(\delta_G,\theta,\gamma,\sigma)$ sweep, ten admit a proper solution and
nine of those ten select the corner; the exception is $\theta=0.80$ at
$\delta_G=0.05$, where $\phi^\ast=0.94$ from the common start, and it
returns to the corner at $\delta_G=0.20$. The four undecidable cells lie
at the perishable end of the stock range ($\delta_G\in\{0.50,1.00\}$)
and yielded no proper profile at the initialisations tried, which is
absence rather than a proof of non-existence.*

*The corner is not a contested instrument. At every one of the nine
computed equilibria — three starts by three weights, each holding its
own equilibrium tax and rate fixed — the capitalist’s own payoff is
strictly increasing in $\phi$ across every admissible split. Of the
$101$ grid splits, $95$ to $98$ are admissible depending on the
equilibrium; the excluded ones are in every case the *lowest\* splits,
where the public stock is starved and the worker’s growth factor falls
through the properness floor of Assumption 5 — at $\phi=0$ exactly, this
is Proposition 2(iii)’s unfunded corner, where the public input is
reproduction-relevant and unfunded and the corner closes by either of
that proposition’s two branches: where the public stock is durable both
dynasties decline at its own rate of depreciation and (N$''$)
contradicts the interiority criterion (A), while at full depreciation
$z_1=0$ and the worker’s growth factor is zero outright. Neither branch
admits an interior rest point. They are therefore inadmissible profiles,
not exceptions to the monotonicity, and the two counts answer different
questions: $95$ to $98$ is how many splits the worker’s dynasty
survives, not how many the capitalist ranks the same way. So his
preferred split is the worker’s chosen corner at each of the nine, and
the agreement is an artefact of neither the weight nor the start. At the
reported cells the worker’s payoff is strictly increasing in $\phi$ on
that same admissible set, so the two classes rank the fiscal split
identically — the orderings coincide, not merely their maximisers — and
the class conflict lives entirely in $(\tau,i)$.\*

*The change of objective moves almost none of this, and that is itself a
finding. Run at the same three starts, the dynastic objective this paper
replaces returns $(0.51,1,0.410)$, $(0.60,1,0.395)$ and
$(0.72,1,0.350)$. Two of the three profiles are identical; the third
differs by one grid atom in each of $\tau$ and $i$. The reason is
Corollary A.1: the worker’s block never saw the capitalist’s payoff, so
his best response is unchanged by construction, and at $\omega=0$ the
capitalist’s ranking over rates turns out to be nearly the same whether
he is maximising his numbers or his consumption. The repair removes an
implausible primitive without disturbing the positive content built on
it — which is what makes the results of Sections 3–5 transplantable
rather than merely re-derivable.*

*What the weight moves is the rate, and it moves it a great deal. At the
canonical start, $i^\ast$ rises from $0.390$ at $\omega=0$ to $0.485$ at
$\omega=0.25$ and $0.610$ at $\omega=0.50$, while $\tau^\ast$ falls only
from $0.61$ to $0.60$ to $0.58$ and the corner holds throughout. So
$\omega$ is not a weakly identified nuisance parameter: it moves the
equilibrium reinvestment rate close to one-for-one and a calibration
cannot leave it free. The private gap falls with it, from $1.96$ to
$1.85$ to $1.72$ at that start, because a more accumulation-minded
capitalist consumes less of his own share.*

Two margins move the corner and $\gamma$ is not one of them. Raising
$\theta$ at $\delta_G=0.05$ flips it; so does lowering $\delta_G$ at
$\theta=0.8$, the same cell being interior at $\delta_G=0.05$ and
returning to the corner at $\delta_G=0.20$. At $\delta_G=0.50$ and
$1.00$ that cell yielded no proper solution at the initialisations
tried, so it decides nothing either way there. A more perishable public
stock makes the worker more willing to fund it to the corner, which is
not the obvious direction; Proposition 3 says why, and shows the
direction is forced. The mechanism behind the capitalist’s agreement is
a one-step elasticity, $\partial \ln c_C'/\partial \ln z =
(1-\alpha)\gamma(1-\theta) > 0$: the reproduction boost public provision
buys dilutes capitalists per worker one-for-one, while only the
$\alpha$-fraction of the capital dilution passes into output.

The corner is not a golden-rule outcome, and the $\delta_G$ margin is
what shows it. Comparing balanced-growth paths cannot produce either
margin. Holding $(\tau,i)$ fixed, the split enters the stationary system
through a single scalar,
$$\Phi(\phi) = \big[(1-\alpha)+(1-\phi)\tau\alpha\big]^{\theta}\,(\phi\tau\alpha)^{1-\theta}, \tag{4}$$
because substituting $x(g)$ and $z(g)$ into $\ln g=\ln g_W$ leaves the
split entering only through $\Phi$: the balanced-growth condition is
$H_0(g)=\gamma\ln\Phi(\phi)$, where $H_0$ collects the $\phi$-free part
of Theorem 2(ii)’s $H$, so that $H_0'=H'>0$ by that theorem. $H_0$ is
therefore strictly increasing, hence invertible, and $g^\ast$ is a
strictly increasing function of $\Phi$; two different splits sharing a
value of $\Phi$ deliver the same balanced growth. Since $\ln\Phi$ is
strictly concave, the growth-maximising split is
$$\phi_g = \frac{(1-\theta)\big[(1-\alpha)+\tau\alpha\big]}{\tau\alpha}, \tag{5}$$
free of $\beta,\ \delta,\ \delta_G,\ \gamma,\ \sigma$ and $i$.

Equation (5) is a benchmark rather than the worker’s choice, and the gap
is the point. At every cell of the sweep $\phi_g>1$, so the
balanced-growth comparison predicts the corner at every one of them,
including at the one cell where the worker in fact chooses an interior
split — $0.94$ from the common start, $0.84$ solved as a self-consistent
stationary equilibrium at that cell’s own balanced-growth point. And (5)
is identically independent of $\delta_G$, while the worker’s best
response moves from $0.768$ to the corner across
$\delta_G\in\{0.05,0.20,0.50,1.00\}$. Stock durability cannot move the
long-run optimum; what it moves is the discounted cost of building the
stock. The corner is a fact about the transition, not about the balanced
growth the transition leads to, which is also why the sweep verdicts are
unchanged when each cell is re-run at its own balanced-growth point.

Charging the worker for building the stock turns that benchmark into a
decision rule. Hold the tax and the reinvestment rate at their
equilibrium values. The envelope calculation of Appendix B gives the
marginal value of the split, at a balanced-growth point, the sign of
$$\Psi(\phi) \;=\; \frac{(1-\theta)\Omega}{\phi} \;-\; \frac{\theta\tau\alpha}{\kappa_W(\phi)},
\qquad \Omega \;=\; \frac{\tilde\beta\,(g-1+\delta_G)}{g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)}, \quad
\tilde\beta = \beta g^{1-\sigma}, \tag{6}$$ with
$\kappa_W(\phi) = (1-\alpha)+(1-\phi)\tau\alpha$ the worker’s
consumption share. Equation (6) is the envelope step and is derived, not
assumed. Both symbols are new: $\Psi$ is a marginal value and takes
either sign, distinct from the positive reproduction scale $B$ of
Assumption 2, and $\Omega$ is the transition wedge, distinct from the
capital stock $K$.

One regularity condition is worth flagging here rather than leaving to
the appendix, because the dependency runs forward. Every envelope step
differentiates a value function with respect to a state, and Assumptions
1–5 do not state that $V_W$ and $V_C$ are differentiable. Within the
constant-share class it need not be assumed: the value functions are
explicit sums of real-analytic terms, and the differentiated series is
dominated near an interior rest point provided the reduced Jacobian’s
spectrum lies strictly inside the unit disc — which is what **Theorem
2(iii) of Section 7** supplies. So this section rests on the next one,
on a neighbourhood of a rest point and on constant shares only; the
argument and its three limits are in the appendix.

**Proposition 3 (the transition wedge, and the unique stationary
split).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\theta\in(0,1)$, and let $g$
be the balanced-growth factor at which (6) is evaluated. Part (i) needs
nothing further. Parts (ii)–(iii) assume in addition that the worker’s
problem is proper, $\tilde\beta<1$ (Assumption 5) — which by (i) is
exactly what puts $\Omega$ in $(0,1)$, and which (i) shows is not
implied by Assumptions 1–4. Then:*

*(i) **The wedge is a proper fraction exactly under properness.***
$$\Omega \in (0,1) \iff \tilde\beta<1 \iff \beta g^{1-\sigma}<1,$$
*which is the worker’s own admissibility condition in Assumption 5.
Assumptions 1–4 alone do not deliver even $\Omega>0$: writing
$g/(1-\delta_G)=+\infty$ when $\delta_G=1$, the wedge equals $1$ at
$\tilde\beta=1$, exceeds $1$ on $1<\tilde\beta<g/(1-\delta_G)$, is
undefined at $\tilde\beta=g/(1-\delta_G)$, and is negative above it.
Moreover, wherever $\Omega$ is defined, at fixed $g$,*
$$\frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial \delta_G} \;=\; \frac{\tilde\beta\,g\,(1-\tilde\beta)}
        {\big(g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)\big)^{2}} \;>\;0
\quad\text{exactly when }\tilde\beta<1,$$ *so $\Omega$ rises with the
public stock’s depreciation, again precisely on the proper region.*

*(ii) **$\Psi$ crosses zero once, and from above.** $\Psi$ is strictly
decreasing on $(0,1]$, with*
$$\Psi'(\phi) \;=\; -\frac{(1-\theta)\Omega}{\phi^{2}} \;-\; \frac{\theta\tau^{2}\alpha^{2}}{\kappa_W(\phi)^{2}}
\;<\;0,$$ *and $\Psi(\phi)\to+\infty$ as $\phi\to0^{+}$. Hence $\Psi$
has at most one zero in $(0,1]$, and where it has one it changes sign
from positive to negative.*

*(iii) **The stationary split is unique and explicit.** It is the corner
$\phi^\dagger=1$ when*
$$\tau \;\le\; \frac{(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)\Omega}{\theta\alpha},$$ *and
otherwise the unique interior root*
$$\phi^\dagger \;=\; \frac{(1-\theta)\Omega\big[(1-\alpha)+\tau\alpha\big]}
                          {\tau\alpha\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]} \;\in\;(0,1). \tag{7}$$

*Proof.* **(i)** Write $N=\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta_G)$ and
$D=g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)$, so $\Omega=N/D$. By Assumption 4,
$g>1-\delta_G$, and $\tilde\beta>0$, so $N>0$ throughout: the sign of
$\Omega$ is the sign of $D$, and Assumptions 1–4 do not sign $D$.

*Proper side.* Let $\tilde\beta<1$. When $\delta_G<1$,
$D > (1-\delta_G)-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G) = (1-\delta_G)(1-\tilde\beta) > 0$;
when $\delta_G=1$, $D = g > 0$. Either way $D>0$, so $\Omega>0$, and
because $D>0$,
$$\Omega<1 \iff \tilde\beta(g-1+\delta_G) < g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)
      \iff \tilde\beta g-\tilde\beta+\tilde\beta\delta_G < g-\tilde\beta+\tilde\beta\delta_G
      \iff \tilde\beta g<g,$$ which holds since $g>0$. Hence
$\Omega\in(0,1)$.

*Improper side.* Subtracting and collecting gives the identity
$\Omega-1 = g(\tilde\beta-1)/D$ wherever $D\neq0$. At $\tilde\beta=1$ it
returns $\Omega=1$, and $D=g-(1-\delta_G)>0$ there by Assumption 4, so
the wedge is defined. Let $\tilde\beta>1$. If $\delta_G=1$ then $D=g>0$
and $\Omega=\tilde\beta>1$. If $\delta_G<1$ then $D>0$, $D=0$ or $D<0$
according as $\tilde\beta$ lies below, at, or above the threshold
$g/(1-\delta_G)$, which exceeds $1$ by Assumption 4. Where $D>0$ the
identity gives $\Omega>1$; where $D=0$ the wedge is undefined; where
$D<0$ the sign of $\Omega$ is the sign of $D$, so $\Omega<0$. So
$\Omega\in(0,1)$ if and only if $\tilde\beta<1$, and no hypothesis
weaker than properness delivers even $\Omega>0$: at $\beta=0.8192$,
$\sigma=5$, $g=0.8$, $\delta=1$, $\delta_G=0.5$ — Assumptions 1–4 all
satisfied — $\tilde\beta=\beta g^{1-\sigma}=2$ and $\Omega=-3$.

For the derivative, take $D\neq0$ and differentiate the quotient at
fixed $\tilde\beta$ and $g$: the numerator’s derivative in $\delta_G$ is
$\tilde\beta$ and the denominator’s is $\tilde\beta$, so
$$\frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial \delta_G}
 = \frac{\tilde\beta\big(g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)\big)-\tilde\beta^{2}(g-1+\delta_G)}
        {\big(g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)\big)^{2}}
 = \frac{\tilde\beta\big(g-\tilde\beta g\big)}{\big(g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)\big)^{2}}
 = \frac{\tilde\beta g(1-\tilde\beta)}{\big(g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)\big)^{2}},$$
the middle step because $-\tilde\beta+\tilde\beta\delta_G$ cancels
against $+\tilde\beta-\tilde\beta\delta_G$. This is positive if and only
if $\tilde\beta<1$.

**(ii)** $\kappa_W$ is affine in $\phi$ with
$\kappa_W(0)=(1-\alpha)+\tau\alpha>0$ and $\kappa_W(1)=1-\alpha>0$,
hence $\kappa_W>0$ on $[0,1]$ and $\Psi$ is differentiable there. Since
$\kappa_W'(\phi)=-\tau\alpha$,
$$\Psi'(\phi) = -\frac{(1-\theta)\Omega}{\phi^{2}} + \theta\tau\alpha\,\frac{\kappa_W'(\phi)}{\kappa_W(\phi)^{2}}
           = -\frac{(1-\theta)\Omega}{\phi^{2}} - \frac{\theta\tau^{2}\alpha^{2}}{\kappa_W(\phi)^{2}},$$
the first term strictly negative because $\theta\in(0,1)$ and $\Omega>0$
by (i) under the standing properness hypothesis of parts (ii)–(iii), and
the second non-positive, with equality only at $\tau=0$, where $\Psi$
has no zero to begin with. A strictly decreasing function has at most
one zero and can only cross from above. As $\phi\to0^+$ the first term
diverges to $+\infty$ while the second stays bounded (it tends to
$\theta\tau\alpha/[(1-\alpha)+\tau\alpha]$), so $\Psi(\phi)\to+\infty$.

**(iii)** By (ii), $\Psi\ge0$ on all of $(0,1]$ — so the one-shot
objective is non-decreasing throughout, strictly increasing on $(0,1)$,
and the maximiser is the corner — if and only if $\Psi(1)\ge0$. Now
$\Psi(1)=(1-\theta)\Omega-\theta\tau\alpha/(1-\alpha)$, and multiplying
by $1-\alpha>0$,
$$\Psi(1)\ge0 \iff (1-\theta)\Omega(1-\alpha)\ \ge\ \theta\tau\alpha
          \iff \tau \le \frac{(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)\Omega}{\theta\alpha},$$
which is the stated corner condition. If instead $\Psi(1)<0$, then
$\Psi$ is continuous and strictly decreasing on $(0,1]$ with
$\Psi(0^+)=+\infty>0>\Psi(1)$, so by the intermediate value theorem it
has exactly one zero $\phi^\dagger\in(0,1)$. Solving $\Psi(\phi)=0$,
$$\frac{(1-\theta)\Omega}{\phi}=\frac{\theta\tau\alpha}{\kappa_W(\phi)}
\iff (1-\theta)\Omega\big[(1-\alpha)+\tau\alpha-\phi\tau\alpha\big]=\theta\tau\alpha\phi
\iff (1-\theta)\Omega\big[(1-\alpha)+\tau\alpha\big]=\phi\,\tau\alpha\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big],$$
which rearranges to (7). $\blacksquare$

Proposition 3 holds $g$ fixed, while the split also moves $g$ and hence
$\Omega$; applying its condition to behaviour therefore requires the
one-shot marginal value to determine the direction of the best response.
At a profile evaluated at the rest point it induces, the derivative of
the worker’s discounted payoff carries the sign of $\Psi$ computed at
that profile’s own growth factor — the transition, the workforce
dilution and the movement in $g$ are all inside $\Omega$ already, which
is what Proposition 9 shows $\Omega$ to be. What the third channel still
governs is where the crossing sits as $\phi$ varies, not whether the
margin’s sign is the payoff’s, and that is the question Corollary 2
reduces to a criterion.

**Corollary 1 (the benchmark is the rule at $\Omega=1$, and
over-predicts below it).** *Under the hypotheses of Proposition 3(iii),
the growth-maximising split (5) and the stationary split (7) are the
same expression evaluated at two values of the transition wedge:*
$$\phi^\dagger\big|_{\Omega=1} \;=\; \phi_g,
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\frac{\partial \phi^\dagger}{\partial \Omega}
  \;=\; \frac{(1-\theta)\,\theta\,\big[(1-\alpha)+\tau\alpha\big]}
             {\tau\alpha\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]^{2}} \;>\;0 .$$
*Hence $\phi^\dagger < \phi_g$ **strictly** whenever $\Omega\in(0,1)$,
which by Proposition 3(i) is exactly when the worker’s problem is
proper. The balanced-growth benchmark therefore over-predicts the split
wherever the comparison is defined at all; it does not merely happen
to.*

*Proof.* Setting $\Omega=1$ in (7) gives
$(1-\theta)\big[(1-\alpha)+\tau\alpha\big]\big/\big(\tau\alpha[\theta+1-\theta]\big)
= (1-\theta)\big[(1-\alpha)+\tau\alpha\big]/(\tau\alpha)$, which is (5).
For the derivative, write
$\phi^\dagger = (1-\theta)\Omega M\big/\big(\tau\alpha[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega]\big)$
with $M=(1-\alpha)+\tau\alpha>0$. Differentiating the quotient in
$\Omega$, the numerator of the derivative is
$(1-\theta)M\,\tau\alpha[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega] - (1-\theta)\Omega M\,\tau\alpha(1-\theta)
= (1-\theta)M\tau\alpha\,\theta$, so
$\partial\phi^\dagger/\partial \Omega = (1-\theta)\theta M\big/\big(\tau\alpha[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega]^2\big)$,
strictly positive because $\theta\in(0,1)$ and $M,\tau,\alpha>0$ — and
the quotient is differentiable at every such $\Omega$ because
$\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega>\theta>0$ there. Strict monotonicity on $(0,1]$
plus $\phi^\dagger|_{\Omega=1}=\phi_g$ gives $\phi^\dagger<\phi_g$ for
$\Omega\in(0,1)$, and Proposition 3(i) identifies $\Omega\in(0,1)$ with
$\tilde\beta<1$. $\blacksquare$

So $\Omega$ is not merely correlated with the gap between benchmark and
rule: it is the gap, the cost of the transition compressed into one
number, and the over-prediction is tied to admissibility by an identity
rather than by a computation. The direction of that over-prediction is
not this paper’s finding — Futagami, Morita and Shibata (1993)
established it for public capital in production, and Section 9 places
the result against theirs. What is this model’s own is the equivalence
itself. That $\Omega$ rises with $\delta_G$, again exactly under
properness, settles the direction that looked wrong above. The
elasticity of the public stock to the split builds up as $1-c^{t}$ in
Assumption 4’s own survival share $c=(1-\delta_G)/g$, so a durable stock
is a slow-moving one: when $\delta_G$ is small the stock is large
relative to the flow that feeds it, a change in the flow moves the ratio
only gradually, and the worker waits longer for the same eventual gain.
Perishability buys delivery. Finally $\Psi\to+\infty$ as $\phi\to0^{+}$
by Proposition 3(ii), so no parameterization makes zero public provision
attractive: the corner the properness mask excludes is one no worker
would choose in any case. Proposition 2(iii) says the same thing in
levels rather than at the margin, and says it without an envelope: at
$\phi=0$ with $\theta<1$ the worker’s dynasty contracts at $\delta_G$
whatever he taxes, and where $\delta_G=1$ it stops in one period.

The same envelope applied to the tax needs one object more, because
unlike the split the tax moves capital:
$\partial K_{t+1}/\partial\tau = -i\alpha Y$. Writing $a_j$ for the
marginal value of a state times that state, the capital envelope at a
balanced-growth point gives
$a_K/a_{N_W} = \tilde\beta\alpha g\gamma\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]/D_x$
with $D_x = g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta)-\tilde\beta\alpha(g-1+\delta)$, and
the tax condition is
$$\frac{\theta(1-\phi)}{\kappa_W(\phi)} \;+\; \frac{(1-\theta)\Omega}{\tau\alpha}
  \;=\; \frac{J\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]}{1-\tau},
\qquad J \;=\; \frac{\tilde\beta\,(g-1+\delta)}{D_x}. \tag{8}$$

**Proposition 4 (the worker’s stationary policy pair).** *Let
Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\theta\in(0,1)$, and let $\Omega$ be as in
(6) and $J$ as in (8), with $D_x\neq0$ so that $J$ is defined. Parts
(i)–(ii) are algebraic identities and need nothing further; part (iii)
assumes in addition that the worker’s problem is proper, $\tilde\beta<1$
(Assumption 5), which by Proposition 3(i) puts $\Omega$ in $(0,1)$.
Then:*

*(i) **$J$ is the capital-side twin of $\Omega$.** Setting $\alpha=0$ in
the formula for $J$ — a limit taken on the expression, $\alpha=0$ being
outside Assumption 1 — leaves exactly $\Omega$ with $\delta$ in place of
$\delta_G$.*

*(ii) **Neither wedge contains $\gamma$.** $\Omega$ and $J$ are
functions of $(\tilde\beta,g,\delta_G)$ and
$(\tilde\beta,g,\delta,\alpha)$ respectively; the reproduction
elasticity enters the worker’s conditions only through $g$.*

*(iii) **At the corner the tax is explicit, and $D_x>0$ costs nothing
extra.** Properness alone forces the capital denominator positive,
because*
$$D_x \;=\; (g-1+\delta)\,(1-\tilde\beta\alpha) \;+\; (1-\delta)\,(1-\tilde\beta) \;>\; 0,$$
*so $J>0$ and $D_x>0$ is a consequence rather than a hypothesis. At
$\phi=1$ the first term of (8) vanishes and the condition solves
uniquely for*
$$\tau^\ast \;=\; \frac{(1-\theta)\Omega}{(1-\theta)\Omega \;+\; J\alpha\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]}
\;\in\;(0,1). \tag{9}$$ *This is the unique interior root of the
unconstrained condition, and it is the worker’s stationary tax only
where it also satisfies the instrument ceiling of Assumption 1,
$\tau^\ast\le\bar\tau$; where it does not, the constrained optimum is
the corner $\bar\tau$ and (9) is not attained. Nothing in Assumptions
1–5 forces $\tau^\ast\le\bar\tau$ — that the ceiling is slack at the
reported cells is a computed fact, not a property of the model.*

*Proof.* **(i)** Setting $\alpha=0$ in $D_x$ leaves
$D_x=g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta)$, so
$J=\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta)/\big(g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta)\big)$, which is
the expression for $\Omega$ in (6) with $\delta$ substituted for
$\delta_G$. **(ii)** Immediate from the two definitions: $\gamma$
appears in neither, and $g$ is the only channel by which a reproduction
parameter can enter. **(iii)** For the decomposition of $D_x$, write
$u=g-1+\delta$ and $v=1-\delta$, so that $u+v=g$, $u>0$ by Assumption 4
and $v\in[0,1)$ by Assumption 1. Then
$D_x = g-\tilde\beta v-\tilde\beta\alpha u = u(1-\tilde\beta\alpha)+v(1-\tilde\beta)$,
and under $\tilde\beta<1$ with $\alpha\in(0,1)$ both coefficients are
strictly positive, so $D_x>0$ and hence $J=\tilde\beta u/D_x>0$. This
step uses Assumptions 1 and 4 together with properness and nothing else
— in particular it does not use (8), which is why Lemma B.1 may invoke
it. The remainder of (iii) does use (8) and therefore stands downstream
of that lemma. At $\phi=1$, $1-\phi=0$, so the first term of (8) is zero
whatever $\kappa_W(1)=1-\alpha>0$ is, and (8) reduces to
$$\frac{(1-\theta)\Omega}{\tau\alpha}=\frac{J\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]}{1-\tau}.$$
Cross-multiplying,
$(1-\theta)\Omega(1-\tau)=\tau\alpha J\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]$,
so
$(1-\theta)\Omega=\tau\big\{(1-\theta)\Omega+J\alpha[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega]\big\}$,
and dividing by the brace — which is strictly positive because
$\Omega>0$ by Proposition 3(i) under the properness hypothesis, $J>0$ as
just shown, and $\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega>0$ — gives (9). The solution is
unique because the equation is linear in $\tau$ with a non-zero
coefficient. It lies in $(0,1)$ because numerator and denominator are
positive and the denominator exceeds the numerator by
$J\alpha[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega]>0$. $\blacksquare$

Part (ii) is what the $\gamma$ sweep of Computation 1 measured: because
neither wedge carries $\gamma$, the worker’s tax is independent of the
elasticity of reproduction except through $g$, which is why sweeping
$\gamma$ moves $\tau^\ast$ by at most one grid atom. What was a measured
regularity is here a property of the condition. The tax rises with the
weight on the reproduction stock’s build-up and falls with the weight on
capital’s — the worker’s side of the conflict in two symbols.

9)  says more than that direction, and the extra is worth stating
    because it is what keeps the tax interior. Rearranged,
    $$1-\tau^\ast \;=\; \frac{J\alpha\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]}
                        {(1-\theta)\Omega \;+\; J\alpha\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]},$$
    so the whole of the distance between the worker’s stationary tax and
    full expropriation is the capital wedge. Set $J=0$ and the condition
    returns $\tau^\ast=1$ — a boundary Assumption 4 excludes, $J=0$
    requiring $g=1-\delta$, which is why the statement is a
    decomposition of the gap rather than a case. Nothing outside the
    worker’s own problem is keeping him off the corner: part (iii)
    records that nothing in Assumptions 1–5 forces
    $\tau^\ast\le\bar\tau$, and at the reported cells the ceiling is
    slack, so what stops him is the price his own condition puts on the
    capital his tax reduces. That price is not a foreign object either —
    by (i) it is the public wedge read on the other stock, $\Omega$ with
    $\delta$ in place of $\delta_G$ — so the two forces in the last
    paragraph are one formula evaluated at the two depreciation rates.
    And the pair recovers the corner’s own policy: at $\theta=1$ — a
    limit taken on the expression, $\theta=1$ being outside Proposition
    3’s range — the bracket in (6) is $-\tau\alpha/\kappa_W<0$ at every
    split, so the condition drives $\phi$ to zero, predicting the pure
    transfer rather than assuming it. Proposition 2 established the
    nesting for the steady state; the same restriction now also recovers
    the corner’s policy. The reinvestment rate $i$ is an input to
    (7)–(9), which are therefore the worker’s pair of stationary
    conditions and not yet the equilibrium. Closing it needs the
    capitalist’s envelope, and under (O) that envelope runs over the
    worker’s own three states.

A convention, once, because two classes could in principle discount at
two rates. **From here to the end of Section 8 the two curvatures are
taken equal**, $\sigma_W=\sigma_C=\sigma$, so that
$\tilde\beta=\beta g^{1-\sigma}$ is one number and the wedges
$$\Omega=\frac{\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta_G)}{g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)},\qquad
  D_x=g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta)-\tilde\beta\alpha(g-1+\delta),\qquad
  J=\frac{\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta)}{D_x}$$ are one family rather than
two. This is a restriction for readability and not a requirement of the
model: Proposition A.1 proves the state reduction *per player*, at each
class own exponent, so every result below holds verbatim with
$\tilde\beta_j=\beta g^{1-\sigma_j}$ carried through and each object
read at the exponent of the class whose condition it appears in. Nothing
would change but the subscripts, and Appendix B keeps them. The one
place the distinction does real work is Assumption 5, whose two clauses
differ off a rest point whatever the curvatures, because the capitalist
payoff object grows at the worker factor while his own reproduction rate
does not enter it at all.

Under (O) the capitalist’s value function is $V_C(K,G,N_W)$ — the
worker’s own state list, with $N_C$ gone. That is the first consequence
of the change of objective and it removes a state from the game: $N_C$
enters neither output, nor either stock law, nor the worker’s
reproduction (Corollary A.1), and now it enters nothing the capitalist
values either. Two further differences follow from (O) and both are
visible in one place. His flow payoff carries $K$ and $N_W$, where the
dynastic one carried neither, so the capital and workforce envelopes
each acquire a flow term while the stock envelope does not — $G$ is
absent from his felicity, which is the class asymmetry again and is why
(B.3) is the worker’s verbatim. The elasticities of that payoff are
$$\frac{\partial \ln P}{\partial \ln K} \;=\; \zeta \;\equiv\; \alpha+\omega(1-\alpha)
\;\in\;[\alpha,1),
\qquad
\frac{\partial \ln P}{\partial \ln N_W} \;=\; (1-\omega)(1-\alpha),
\qquad
\frac{\partial \ln P}{\partial \ln G} \;=\; 0,$$ which sum to one —
Euler on a degree-one aggregate — and leave the public stock outside his
felicity, so the paper’s class asymmetry survives the change intact. The
symbol $\zeta$ is new and is used below only in the capitalist’s
envelopes; it is distinct from the stock poles $\mu$ and $\mu_K$ of
Section 8, which are functions of the depreciation rates and carry no
preference parameter.

Write $\varphi \equiv P^{1-\sigma}>0$ for the scale of his flow marginal
value. The rate reaches $K'$ by $(1-\tau)\alpha Y$ and his own
consumption by $-1/(1-i)$, and reaches nothing else, so the condition
again has two terms:
$$\underbrace{\tilde\beta\,\frac{a_K\,(g-1+\delta)}{g\,i}}_{\text{benefit}}
\;=\;\underbrace{\frac{(1-\omega)\,\varphi}{1-i}}_{\text{cost}} . \tag{10}$$
Compare the dynastic condition it replaces,
$a_K/a_{N_C}=i\gamma g/[(1-i)(g-1+\delta)]$: the benefit is unchanged
and the cost has changed hands, from the dilution of his dynasty to the
loss of his own consumption. The factor $1-\omega$ in that cost is the
whole of what the weight does, and it is why $\omega=1$ is excluded.

Two of the three envelopes are worth reading. The stock envelope is the
worker’s verbatim — the same two terms for either class, each read at
its own exponent, so that $\Omega_C$ stands where $\Omega$ does under
class-indexing — because public infrastructure still reaches the
capitalist only through the workforce. The capital envelope is the
worker’s plus a single term:
$$a_K D_x \;=\; \tilde\beta\alpha\gamma g\,a_{N_W}\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)
   \;+\; g\,\zeta\,\varphi, \tag{11}$$ the bare $g\zeta\varphi$ being
capital’s direct claim on his felicity: capital feeds the workforce that
produces *and* enters his payoff on its own account, where for the
worker it did only the first. Delete that term and the worker’s envelope
returns exactly. This is the same slot the own-dynasty term $a_{N_C}$
occupied under the dynastic objective, and it is occupied now by a
different tenant — which is why the results built on (11) change their
reading without changing their form.

The remaining envelope is linear in $(a_K,a_{N_W})$ given $\varphi$, so
with $$\rho \;\equiv\; \frac{a_{N_W}}{\varphi}$$ — the capitalist’s
marginal valuation of the workforce against the scale of his own flow
payoff, itself closed-form, and positive under properness by Lemma B.2 —
the rate is explicit: $$i^\ast \;=\; \frac{\Lambda}{1+\Lambda}, \qquad
  \Lambda \;=\; \frac{J}{1-\omega}\Big[\,\zeta
    \;+\; \tilde\beta\,\alpha\gamma\,\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)\,\rho\,\Big]. \tag{12}$$
At $\omega=0$, where $\zeta=\alpha$, this is
$\Lambda=J\alpha\big[\,r(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega)+1\,\big]$ with
$r=\tilde\beta\gamma\rho$ — the dynastic paper’s own expression, with
the workforce valuation normalised by a flow scale instead of by an
own-dynasty marginal value. The rate condition keeps its algebraic shape
across the change of objective; what changes is what stands in the
denominator of the capitalist’s valuation of the workers.

**Proposition 5 (the reinvestment margin exists exactly for $\omega<1$,
single-crosses, and its zero is interior).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold
and fix $a_K>0$, $\varphi>0$, $g$ and $\delta$ with $g-1+\delta>0$
(Assumption 4). Define on $i\in(0,1)$ the marginal benefit and cost of
(10),*
$$\mathrm{Ben}(i) \;=\; \frac{\tilde\beta\,a_K\,(g-1+\delta)}{g\,i}, \qquad
  \mathrm{Cost}(i) \;=\; \frac{(1-\omega)\,\varphi}{1-i}.$$

*(i) **The upper endpoint of Assumption 3 is a degeneracy, not a
convention.** At $\omega=1$ the cost vanishes identically and
$\partial V_C/\partial i>0$ at every admissible $i$, so the capitalist’s
problem has no first-order condition and $i^\ast=1$ unconditionally. The
same conclusion follows without any envelope: at $\omega=1$ his payoff
object is $K$ alone, the transition in $(K,G,N_W)$ is order-preserving
in $\iota=i(1-\tau)$ — the step Proposition 14’s proof reads off the
transition laws, which needs only that every partial be nonnegative and
that $m=\gamma[\theta\alpha+(1-\theta)]<1$, both from Assumptions 1–2,
so it survives at $\theta=1$ where $m=\gamma\alpha$ and Proposition 14’s
own statement does not reach, its wedge $\Omega^{\rm fix}$ dividing by
$1-\theta$ — and raising the rate raises every date’s capital stock
componentwise with nothing to weigh against it. Both routes give the
corner, and every result below that has $i^\ast$ as its subject is
vacuous there.*

*(ii) **For $\omega<1$ the margin exists and crosses once, from above.**
$\mathrm{Ben}-\mathrm{Cost}$ is strictly decreasing on $(0,1)$, diverges
to $+\infty$ as $i\to0^{+}$ and to $-\infty$ as $i\to1^{-}$. Hence it
has exactly one zero, it crosses from above, and the zero is interior:
the one-shot marginal value is signed away from both extremes. At a
constant-share rest point with $D_x\neq0$, so that $J$ and hence
$\Lambda$ of (12) are defined, that zero is
$i^\ast=\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)$, which lies in $(0,1)$ for every
$\Lambda>0$.*

*(iii) **The exclusion of the upper corner is uniform on closed
subintervals.** The coefficient of the divergence in (ii) is $1-\omega$
itself, so on $\omega\le1-\varepsilon$ it is bounded below by
$\varepsilon>0$: the degeneracy of (i) is a boundary phenomenon and not
a knife edge inside the admissible range.*

*Proof.* **(i)** With $\omega=1$, $\mathrm{Cost}\equiv0$ while
$\mathrm{Ben}>0$ under the stated hypotheses, so the difference is
strictly positive on $(0,1)$ and has no zero. For the envelope-free
route, $\zeta=1$ at $\omega=1$ makes $P=K$; Proposition 14’s proof shows
the transition map in $(K,G,N_W)$ is order-preserving and that $\iota$
enters it monotonically upward, so $K_t$ is non-decreasing in $i$ at
every $t$ and strictly increasing for $t\ge1$. Under (O) with $\omega=1$
nothing enters his payoff that $i$ lowers, so $V_C$ is strictly
increasing in $i$. The monotone step reaches the capitalist here
precisely because it is his own consumption that it failed to reach
before.

**(ii)** $\mathrm{Ben}'(i)=-\tilde\beta a_K(g-1+\delta)/(g\,i^{2})<0$
because $a_K>0$, $g>0$ and $g-1+\delta>0$ by Assumption 4;
$\mathrm{Cost}'(i)=(1-\omega)\varphi/(1-i)^{2}>0$ because $\varphi>0$
and $\omega<1$. Their difference is therefore strictly decreasing, so it
has at most one zero and can only cross from above. As $i\to0^{+}$,
$\mathrm{Ben}\to+\infty$ while $\mathrm{Cost}\to(1-\omega)\varphi$ is
bounded, so the difference tends to $+\infty$; as $i\to1^{-}$,
$\mathrm{Cost}\to+\infty$ while
$\mathrm{Ben}\to\tilde\beta a_K(g-1+\delta)/g$ is bounded, so the
difference tends to $-\infty$. By the intermediate value theorem a zero
exists, and by strict monotonicity it is unique and interior. Setting
$\mathrm{Ben}=\mathrm{Cost}$ is exactly (10); writing its solution as
$i/(1-i)=\Lambda$ and solving gives $i^\ast=\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)$, which
lies strictly between $0$ and $1$ whenever $\Lambda>0$ because then
$0<\Lambda<1+\Lambda$. **(iii)** is immediate from the displayed cost,
whose coefficient is $1-\omega$. $\blacksquare$

Part (i) is worth separating from the rest, because it disposes of the
objective a reader arrives with. Replacing the capitalist’s dynastic
payoff by discounted capital or after-tax wealth is the
economical-looking repair — it removes the implausible symmetry and
leaves the state reduction, the funding structure and the envelope
method standing — and it is exactly $\omega=1$. What it also removes is
the trade-off: with nothing in his payoff that reinvestment lowers,
Proposition 14’s order-preserving argument reaches him, and reaches him
with a corner rather than a criterion. Everything in this paper whose
subject is $i^\ast$ — this proposition, Proposition 10, Proposition 12’s
rate column, Theorem 6 — is then vacuous rather than rebuildable. The
opposite repair fails symmetrically: an objective in the profit rate
$\alpha Y/K$ is decreasing in the stock it accumulates, so it drives
$i^\ast$ to zero, and it is homogeneous of degree zero, which destroys
the reduction outright. The admissible replacements are the ones in
which reinvesting still costs him something he values, and (O) is the
one-parameter family of them that keeps both the homogeneity and the
log-affineness the rest of the paper uses.

Proposition 5 is a statement about the one-shot marginal value of (10)
at a fixed balanced-growth factor, not about the dynamic best response:
$a_K$, $\varphi$ and $g$ are held fixed and all three move with $i$.
That the sign of this marginal value gives the direction of travel of
the constant-share best response is Theorem 4, proved at a start equal
to the induced rest point; what this proposition delivers on its own is
the crossing, and the bridge from it to the payoff is Section 8.4’s. The
proposition takes $a_K>0$ and $\varphi>0$ as hypotheses, and its part
(ii) adds $\Lambda>0$; $\varphi>0$ is immediate from $\sigma>1$, and
Lemma B.2 discharges the other two from Assumptions 1–4 and properness:
at a constant-share rest point the capitalist’s three marginal values
are all strictly positive, which signs $\rho$ and hence $\Lambda$, so
the interiority is a consequence and not a parameter restriction. The
crossing is established exactly as $\Psi(\phi)$’s is in Proposition 3,
and again without any curvature hypothesis: no concavity of $V_C$ in $i$
is used or claimed. What the two proofs share is that each margin is a
difference of a decreasing and an increasing function, which is a
structural fact about where the instrument enters rather than a
regularity assumption.

The optimum is interior, and that is the contrast with the split. The
benefit diverges as $i\to0$ and the cost as $i\to1$, so neither extreme
can zero the one-shot margin; and at the reported cells the marginal
value is positive at the low edge of the admissible range and negative
at the high edge, so the rate settles away from the admissibility
boundary as well. Where $\phi$ sits at its corner in nine of the ten
decidable cells, $i$ is interior in all ten. The two facts carry unequal
weight: the admissible range reaches $i=0$ or $i=1$ at only one cell, so
at the rest it is admissibility rather than the divergence that keeps
the rate away from the extremes.

The two sides share the wedges but not the structure. The transition
wedge and the capital wedge govern both conditions — the same two
objects that price the worker’s transition price the capitalist’s, at
his own discount factor. But (12) carries a third, $\rho$, for which the
worker’s condition has no counterpart: the worker’s own dynasty *is* the
numerator of his payoff, so he never has to price the other class,
whereas the capitalist must price the workforce that produces for him.
The capital envelope differs from the worker’s by exactly the one term
in (11); the condition differs by that term and by $\rho$.

That the capitalist must price the workforce is not an artefact of the
old objective, and it is worth saying why, since $N_C$ has left his
problem and one might expect the cross-class term to leave with it. It
does not, because it never came from his dynasty. It comes from
production: the workforce is the complementary factor in
$Y=AK^{\alpha}N_W^{1-\alpha}$, so more workers raise his output, his
consumption and the stock he accumulates alike. Under the dynastic
objective that channel was overlaid by a second one — more workers meant
more output meant more of his own numbers — and the two were hard to
separate. Under (O) only the production channel survives, and $\rho$
measures it cleanly. The class-asymmetric term in the capitalist’s
condition is therefore a *technological* fact about who produces, not a
demographic one about who reproduces.

The asymmetry is testable, because it breaks the $\gamma$-inertia of the
tax. The tax is inert for the reason Proposition 4(ii) gives; $\rho$, by
contrast, carries $\gamma$ explicitly, so $i^\ast$ should not be inert,
and at the reported cells it is not: sweeping $\gamma$ across
$[0.30,0.70]$ moves $\tau^\ast$ by three tenths of a grid atom and
$i^\ast$ by five, while $\rho$ falls from $6.22$ to $3.26$ — a span of
two thirds of its own mean — against $\Omega$ moving $0.0036$ and $J$
moving $0.0124$. The two wedges are, in relative terms, all but inert in
$\gamma$ — under two per cent of their own level against $\rho$’s
sixty-two. The movement in the rate runs through the capitalist’s
pricing of the class that produces for him, an object with no
worker-side twin.

It does not run through $\rho$ in the direction the reader of (12) will
supply unaided, and the next two results are worth reading together for
that reason: $\rho$ *falls* across the sweep while $i^\ast$ rises. What
(12) contains is not $\rho$ but the product $\gamma\rho$ — equivalently
the $r=\tilde\beta\gamma\rho$ of the $\omega=0$ reading, which rises
from $1.78$ to $2.17$ — and $\gamma$ stands in that product in its own
right as well as inside $\rho$.

That reading is a sweep, and neither direction it reports needs one.

**Proposition 5$'$ (the rate is strictly increasing in the valuation of
the workforce).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\omega<1$, at a
constant-share rest point at which $J$ and $\Omega$ are defined, with
$\Omega>0$ by Proposition 3(i) and $J>0$ under the same properness
hypothesis. Then in (12), holding $(J,\Omega,\zeta,\omega)$ fixed,*
$$\frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial\rho}
  \;=\; \frac{J\,\tilde\beta\alpha\gamma\,\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]}
             {(1-\omega)\,\big(1+\Lambda\big)^{2}} \;>\; 0 .$$

*Proof.* By (12) $\Lambda$ is affine in $\rho$ with slope
$J\tilde\beta\alpha\gamma[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega]/(1-\omega)$. Every
factor is strictly positive: $\tilde\beta,\alpha,\gamma>0$ by
Assumptions 1–2, $\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega>0$ because $\theta\in(0,1)$
and $\Omega>0$, and $1-\omega>0$ by hypothesis. Proposition 5(ii) gives
$i^\ast=\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)$, whose derivative in $\Lambda$ is
$(1+\Lambda)^{-2}>0$. The chain rule gives the display. $\blacksquare$

The economic content is the one the envelope already carried: capital’s
own accumulation rate rises with the price it puts on the workforce it
must reproduce. The derivative is a partial, taken with the two
transition wedges and the weight held fixed, and it must not be read as
a comparative static in a primitive: $\rho$, $J$ and $\Omega$ are each
closed forms in the primitives, so a parameter change that raises $\rho$
moves the others too. For $\gamma$ that total is settled by the next
proposition, and it is settled against the chain a reader would
otherwise complete.

**Proposition 5$''$ (in the reproduction elasticity the valuation falls
and the rate rises).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\omega<1$ at an
**admissible** constant-share rest point — admissible in Assumption 5’s
sense, so that $\tilde\beta<1$, which is what signs $\mathcal{W}$ and
$\eta$ below — and hold the balanced-growth factor $g$ fixed — which by
Proposition 4(ii) is the only channel by which $\gamma$ reaches $J$ or
$\Omega$. By $(\mathrm{B}.7'')$ write
$\mathcal{M}=(1-\tilde\beta)+\gamma\mathcal{W}$ with*
$$\mathcal{W}=\tilde\beta\Big[(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)+\alpha\eta\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)\Big]>0,
\qquad \eta=\frac{g(1-\tilde\beta)}{D_x}=1-(1-\alpha)J\in(0,1) \ \text{ by } (\mathrm{B}.7').$$
*Then, writing $\mathcal{Q}\equiv(1-\omega)+J\zeta>0$ for the
$\gamma$-free bracket that $(\mathrm{B}.7)$ leaves on the right,*
$$\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial\gamma}
   \;=\;-\,\frac{(1-\alpha)\,\mathcal{Q}\,\mathcal{W}}{\mathcal{M}^{2}}\;<\;0,
\qquad
\frac{\partial(\gamma\rho)}{\partial\gamma}
   \;=\;\frac{(1-\alpha)\,\mathcal{Q}\,(1-\tilde\beta)}{\mathcal{M}^{2}}\;>\;0,
\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad
\frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial\gamma}\;>\;0 .$$

*Proof.* $(\mathrm{B}.7)$ reads $\mathcal{M}\rho=(1-\alpha)\mathcal{Q}$,
and neither $\mathcal{Q}$ nor $1-\alpha$ contains $\gamma$.
$(\mathrm{B}.7'')$ writes $\mathcal{M}$ as
$(1-\tilde\beta)+\gamma\mathcal{W}$, and $\mathcal{W}>0$ because
$\Omega\in(0,1)$ by Proposition 3(i), $\eta\in(0,1)$ by
$(\mathrm{B}.7')$ under properness, and $\theta\in(0,1)$.
Differentiating $\rho=(1-\alpha)\mathcal{Q}/\mathcal{M}$ gives the first
display. For the second,
$\gamma\rho=(1-\alpha)\mathcal{Q}\,\gamma/\big[(1-\tilde\beta)+\gamma\mathcal{W}\big]$,
whose derivative is
$(1-\alpha)\mathcal{Q}(1-\tilde\beta)/\mathcal{M}^{2}$: the two
$\gamma\mathcal{W}$ terms cancel and what is left standing in the
numerator is properness itself. In (12) $\gamma$ and $\rho$ occur only
through the product $\gamma\rho$, so $\Lambda$ is affine and strictly
increasing in it, and Proposition 5(ii)’s $i^\ast=\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)$
is a strictly increasing transform of $\Lambda$. $\blacksquare$

Three readings, and the first is why the signs oppose. $\gamma$ reaches
$\rho$ only through $\mathcal{M}$, the net own-feedback of the $\ln N_W$
row: a workforce that reproduces more elastically is cheaper to hold at
the margin, so its shadow price falls — while the elasticity with which
that price reaches the capital envelope rises faster than the price
falls. Marginal value down, total claim up, and it is the total claim
that (12) contains. Second, properness is not hygiene here either:
$1-\tilde\beta$ is the entire numerator of the second display, and at
$\tilde\beta>1$ the rate would fall in $\gamma$ instead. And the
intercept that numerator comes from is not an artifact of Assumption 2 —
Appendix F.1 obtains the same affine structure about the same
$\gamma$-free intercept $1-\tilde\beta$ for an arbitrary composite; what
is established here under Assumption 2 is the sign of the slope. Third,
the scope, which is the honest one: $g$ is held fixed, and $g$ is itself
a fixed point that $\gamma$ moves. That indirect channel is the one
Appendix B.9 declares open. It is a product of two factors, and the next
proposition signs the one that belongs to (12) — the rate falls in the
growth factor — leaving $\mathrm{d}g/\mathrm{d}\gamma$ itself, which is
a property of the outer fixed point and not of the rate condition, and
which Proposition 6$'$ signs there once the share loop is closed.
Freezing $r$ at its midpoint across the sweep and letting only the two
wedges move leaves $0.80$ of the $5.07$ grid atoms standing, so the part
of the indirect channel that travels through $J$ and $\Omega$ is about a
sixth of the rate’s movement at those cells; the part that travels
through $\tilde\beta$ inside $\rho$ is not separated by that
decomposition, and the sixth is a floor on the whole indirect channel
rather than a measure of it.

**Proposition 5$'''$ (the reinvestment bracket collapses, and the rate
falls in the growth factor).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold at an
**admissible** constant-share rest point with $\omega\in[0,1)$ —
admissible in Assumption 5’s sense, so that $\tilde\beta<1$. Properness
is load-bearing three times below and is named here rather than left to
the proof: it is what puts $(1-\alpha)J=1-\eta$ in $(0,1)$ by
$(\mathrm{B}.7')$, what makes $\mathcal{M}>1-\tilde\beta>0$ by
$(\mathrm{B}.7'')$, and what signs $(\sigma-1)+(1-\tilde\beta)>0$ in
(ii). Read every object of (12) as a function of $g$ with the primitives
$(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\sigma,\delta,\delta_G,\theta,\omega)$ held fixed
— a well-defined reading, because neither $\tau$ nor $\varphi$ enters
(12) except through $g$. Write*
$$\mathcal{K}\;=\;(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)\;+\;\alpha\eta\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big),
\qquad
\mathcal{S}\;=\;\frac{\alpha\gamma(1-\alpha)\,\tilde\beta\,J\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)}
                     {\mathcal{M}}\;>\;0,$$ *so that $(\mathrm{B}.7'')$
reads $\mathcal{M}=1-\tilde\beta\big(1-\gamma\mathcal{K}\big)$. Then:*

*(i)*
$1-\mathcal{K}=\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)\in(0,1)$,
*and at $\omega=0$ the reinvestment bracket of (12) collapses to a ratio
of two objects of that same form:*
$$r\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)+1
   \;=\;\frac{1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma)}{1-\tilde\beta\big(1-\gamma\mathcal{K}\big)},
\qquad\text{so}\qquad
\Lambda\;=\;\frac{\alpha J\big[\,1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma)\,\big]}{\mathcal{M}} .$$

*(ii) $\partial J/\partial g<0$ and $\partial\Omega/\partial g<0$ hold
if and only if $(\sigma-1)(g-1+\delta)>(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)$ and
$(\sigma-1)(g-1+\delta_G)>(1-\delta_G)(1-\tilde\beta)$ respectively, and
both follow from the single condition at
$\delta_\wedge=\min\{\delta,\delta_G\}$,*
$$(\sigma-1)\big(g-1+\delta_\wedge\big)\;>\;(1-\delta_\wedge)\big(1-\tilde\beta\big),\tag{$\ddagger$}$$
*under which $\partial\mathcal{K}/\partial g>0$ and
$\partial\mathcal{M}/\partial g>0$. Neither display carries $\omega$.*

*(iii) For every $\omega\in[0,1)$, (12) decomposes as*
$$\Lambda \;=\; \mathcal{S} \;+\; \frac{J\zeta\,\big(1+\mathcal{S}\big)}{1-\omega},
\qquad\text{whence}\qquad
\frac{\partial\Lambda}{\partial g}
  \;=\;\frac{\partial\mathcal{S}}{\partial g}\Big[1+\frac{J\zeta}{1-\omega}\Big]
  \;+\;\frac{\zeta\big(1+\mathcal{S}\big)}{1-\omega}\,\frac{\partial J}{\partial g},$$
*and under $(\ddagger)$ both terms are strictly negative, so*
$$\frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial g}\;<\;0
\qquad\text{at every }\omega\in[0,1).$$

*Proof.* (i) $\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega$ and $(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)$ sum to
one, so
$1-\mathcal{K}=\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)\big(1-\alpha\eta\big)$;
and $(\mathrm{B}.7')$ gives $\eta=1-(1-\alpha)J$, whence
$1-\alpha\eta=(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)$. That factor lies in $(0,1)$
because $(1-\alpha)J=1-\eta\in(0,1)$ is $(\mathrm{B}.7')$ itself; and
$\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\in(0,1]$ because $\Omega\in(0,1)$ by
Proposition 3(i) and $\theta\in(0,1]$ by Assumption 2, the upper end
attained exactly at $\theta=1$, where the product is nonetheless
strictly below one because its other factor is. So
$\mathcal{K}\in(0,1)$, and it is the second factor and not the weight
that keeps it there. At $\omega=0$ the bracket $(\mathrm{B}.7)$ leaves
on the right is $(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)$, so with
$r=\tilde\beta\gamma\rho$ and $\mathcal{M}\rho=(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)$,
$$r\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)
   \;=\;\frac{\tilde\beta\gamma\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)}{\mathcal{M}}
   \;=\;\frac{\tilde\beta\gamma\big(1-\mathcal{K}\big)}{\mathcal{M}} .$$
Adding one and using
$\mathcal{M}=1-\tilde\beta+\tilde\beta\gamma\mathcal{K}$ leaves
$\mathcal{M}+\tilde\beta\gamma(1-\mathcal{K})=1-\tilde\beta+\tilde\beta\gamma$,
which is the first display; the second is
$\Lambda=\alpha J\big[r(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega)+1\big]$.

2)  Proposition 4(iii) writes
    $D_x=(g-1+\delta)(1-\tilde\beta\alpha)+(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)$,
    so $$\frac1J\;=\;\Big(\frac1{\tilde\beta}-\alpha\Big)
       \;+\;\frac{(1-\delta)\big(\tilde\beta^{-1}-1\big)}{g-1+\delta},$$
    and (6) gives the same identity for $1/\Omega$ with $\alpha$ deleted
    and $\delta_G$ in place of $\delta$. Differentiating and clearing
    the strictly positive factor $\tilde\beta^{-1}(g-1+\delta)^{-2}$,
    $$\tilde\beta\,(g-1+\delta)^{2}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial g}\frac1J
       \;=\;(\sigma-1)(g-1+\delta)-(1-\delta)\big(1-\tilde\beta\big),$$
    and likewise for $\Omega$: since $J,\Omega>0$ these are the stated
    equivalences. The map
    $d\mapsto(\sigma-1)(g-1+d)-(1-d)(1-\tilde\beta)$ has derivative
    $(\sigma-1)+(1-\tilde\beta)>0$, so ($\ddagger$) at $\delta_\wedge$
    delivers both. Using $1-\alpha\eta=(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)$ once
    more, $$\frac{\partial\mathcal{K}}{\partial g}
       \;=\;(1-\alpha)\Big[-(1-\theta)(1+\alpha J)\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial g}
     \;-\;\alpha\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)\frac{\partial J}{\partial g}\Big]\;>\;0 ,$$
    and with
    $\partial\tilde\beta/\partial g=(1-\sigma)\beta g^{-\sigma}<0$ under
    Assumption 3, $\partial\mathcal{M}/\partial g
    =-\big(\partial\tilde\beta/\partial g\big)\big(1-\gamma\mathcal{K}\big)
    +\tilde\beta\gamma\,\partial\mathcal{K}/\partial g>0$, both terms
    positive because $\gamma\mathcal{K}<1$. Neither display mentions
    $\omega$, because neither $J$ nor $\Omega$ does.

3)  By $(\mathrm{B}.7)$, $\rho=(1-\alpha)\mathcal{Q}/\mathcal{M}$ with
    $\mathcal{Q}=(1-\omega)+J\zeta$, so the second entry of (12)’s
    bracket is
    $\tilde\beta\alpha\gamma\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)\rho=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}/J$
    and
    $$\Lambda=\frac{J}{1-\omega}\Big[\zeta+\frac{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}}{J}\Big]
      =\frac{J\zeta+\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}}{1-\omega}
      =\mathcal{S}+\frac{J\zeta(1+\mathcal{S})}{1-\omega},$$ the last
    step substituting $\mathcal{Q}=(1-\omega)+J\zeta$. Differentiating
    gives the second display. Now
    $\mathcal{S}=\alpha\gamma(1-\alpha)\cdot\tilde\beta J\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)
    /\mathcal{M}$: under $(\ddagger)$ the three factors of its numerator
    all fall in $g$ — $\tilde\beta$ by Assumption 3, $J$ by (ii), and
    $\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega$ because $\partial\Omega/\partial g<0$ —
    while $\mathcal{M}$ rises by (ii). So
    $\partial\mathcal{S}/\partial g<0$ **term by term, with no
    cancellation**. Since $\zeta,J,\mathcal{S}>0$ and $\omega<1$, the
    bracket $1+J\zeta/(1-\omega)$ is positive and
    $\partial J/\partial g<0$, so both terms of the second display are
    strictly negative. Finally $i^\ast=\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)$ is a
    strictly increasing transform of $\Lambda$.

At $\omega=0$ the same conclusion also follows from (i) alone, by a
cancellation worth recording: differentiating that ratio, the
$\tilde\beta$ cross terms vanish identically and what remains is signed
termwise,
$$\frac{\partial}{\partial g}\,\frac{1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma)}{\mathcal{M}}
   \;=\;\frac{\gamma}{\mathcal{M}^{2}}
   \Big[\frac{\partial\tilde\beta}{\partial g}\big(1-\mathcal{K}\big)
   -\big(1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma)\big)\,\tilde\beta\,\frac{\partial\mathcal{K}}{\partial g}\Big]
   \;<\;0 ,$$ so there $\Lambda$ is a product of two strictly positive,
strictly decreasing factors. $\blacksquare$

Three readings, and then the scope. It is worth saying first why (i)
survives at all, since (iii) proves more with less: (iii) needs only
that four objects move monotonically, and gets the whole weight range
$\omega\in[0,1)$ for it, while (i) buys a *closed form* and buys it at
the reported weight alone. Generality and sharpness are not the same
purchase, and each display is kept for the one it makes. The sign, where
they overlap, is the same sign.

First, what (i) removes. The bracket wears $\theta$, $\Omega$, $\rho$
and $r$ on its face and none of them survives: they enter only through
$\mathcal{K}$, and $1-\mathcal{K}$ is the workforce weight
$\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega$ times the share of the $\ln N_W$ row that
capital’s own feedback leaves unclaimed. Numerator and denominator are
the same object $1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma x)$ at $x=1$ and at
$x=\mathcal{K}<1$, so the premium the bracket carries over one is
exactly the room between them, and it closes as $\mathcal{K}\to1$ —
which is where the workforce weight vanishes. The same form returns
Proposition 5$''$ by a route that never mentions $\rho$: at fixed $g$
neither $J$ nor $\Omega$ nor $\mathcal{K}$ carries $\gamma$, by
Proposition 4(ii), and
$\mathcal{M}-\mathcal{K}\big[1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma)\big]=(1-\tilde\beta)(1-\mathcal{K})$,
so the $\gamma$-derivative of the ratio is
$\tilde\beta(1-\tilde\beta)(1-\mathcal{K})/\mathcal{M}^{2}>0$ —
properness in the numerator again, in a second derivation that shares no
step with the first.

Second, the condition. Both wedges are resolvents of stock laws, and
(ii) says exactly when a faster growth factor shrinks them: the growth
term $(\sigma-1)(g-1+\delta)$ must beat the impatience term
$(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)$. At $\sigma=2$ that is a quadratic in $g$
with discriminant $(1-\delta_\wedge)\big[(1-\delta_\wedge)-\beta\big]$,
so ($\ddagger$) holds at *every* admissible $g$ exactly when
$\beta\ge1-\delta_\wedge$ — at $\beta=1-\delta_\wedge$ the double root
sits exactly at the admissibility floor, which admissibility excludes,
so the margin stays strictly positive there too; the reported cell has
$\beta=0.96$ against $1-\delta_G=0.95$, and it is the infrastructure
rate, not capital’s, that binds. Neither the condition nor its
conclusion is decorative. At a depreciation floor the margin is exactly
$-(1-\delta_\wedge)(1-\tilde\beta)$, so ($\ddagger$) fails — and there
§8.3’s boundary analysis has $\Lambda\to0$, which with $\Lambda\to0$ at
$g\to\infty$ forbids monotonicity outright; at the properness pole
$\tilde\beta=1$ annihilates the right-hand side, so ($\ddagger$) holds —
and there $\Lambda$ diverges. Which of the three floors binds and
whether ($\ddagger$) can hold are the same dichotomy. At $\beta=0.90$
with the reported cell’s other primitives both limbs fail and
$\partial i^\ast/\partial g=+0.196$; at $\beta=0.935$, where only the
$\delta_G$ limb fails, the rate still falls, so ($\ddagger$) is
sufficient and not necessary.

Third, the scope, which is what the two propositions buy together.
Proposition 5$''$ signs $\partial i^\ast/\partial\gamma$ at fixed $g$;
this one signs $\partial i^\ast/\partial g$ at fixed primitives; and the
total the sweep reports is $$\frac{\mathrm{d}i^\ast}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}
   \;=\;\frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial\gamma}\bigg|_{g}
   \;+\;\frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial g}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}g}{\mathrm{d}\gamma},$$
whose second factor belongs to the outer fixed point in $(\tau,i)$
rather than to the rate condition and is not signed here. What the pair
establishes on its own is sharper than either alone: the two channels
**oppose** wherever $\gamma$ raises $g$, and the total carries
Proposition 5$''$’s sign outright wherever it does not. Proposition 6$'$
then says where that is — the growth factor rises in the elasticity at
every fixed point with $g\ge B$ whose share loop has gain below one — so
the opposing case is the one that obtains above the reproduction scale,
and the reinforcing case below it. At the reported cell
$\mathrm{d}g/\mathrm{d}\gamma=+0.0077$ and the direct term $+0.0757$
beats the indirect $-0.0188$; that indirect share is a third of the
total, which is above the sixth the frozen-$r$ decomposition bounds it
by, as a floor requires.

Those magnitudes belong to one cell, but the asymmetry itself needs no
sweep and does not need the Cobb–Douglas composite. Write the worker’s
reproduction as $g_W=F\big(h(c_W,z)\big)$ for an arbitrary increasing
$F$ and an arbitrary composite $h$. The split trades two channels that
both reach $g_W$ through the single scalar $h$, so $F$’s elasticity
$\varepsilon_F$ multiplies both alike, and at every $\phi$ the marginal
value carries the sign of a bracket in which $F$ does not appear: the
crossing is invariant to the whole outer map, and $\gamma$ — which lives
in $F$ — goes with it. It cannot cancel from $r$. Writing $s_c,s_z$ for
the composite’s logarithmic shares, the worker population’s own law of
motion contributes
$$\frac{\partial\ln N_W'}{\partial\ln N_W} \;=\; 1-\varepsilon_F(s_c\alpha+s_z),$$
whose leading $1$ carries no elasticity at all, so there the
reproduction elasticity enters additively rather than multiplicatively.
That is the whole asymmetry, and a CES composite, a subsistence floor
and a saturating map leave it standing alike.

What the cancellation does need is a single index — the $x_W$ of Lemma 1
— rather than a particular form: both channels must reach reproduction
through the same scalar, and attaching $\gamma$ to consumption alone
leaves it in the split. Since a single index is also what makes *the*
elasticity of reproduction a well-defined object, that requirement is
nearer a definition than an assumption. What the Cobb–Douglas form buys
is the closed form, not the asymmetry: under it the composite’s shares
$(s_c,s_z)$ are the constants $(\theta,1-\theta)$, which is what makes
(7) explicit in $\theta$, while under CES they become state-dependent
and (7) survives in shape with a moving share in place of $\theta$.

With (12) the equilibrium is closed within the constant-share class.
These are stationary first-order conditions for share-valued strategies,
and nothing here bears on Markov feedback strategies. What the closed
form should and should not reproduce is decided in advance by Definition
1, and the computation bears it out. Equation (12) is evaluated at the
rest point a profile *induces*; the grid best response maximises the
path payoff from a *declared* start. Where the two coincide the closed
form should be exact, and where the start is far from the rest point it
should not be. Measured at the three starts, the gap
$|i^\ast_{\text{grid}}-i^\ast_{(12)}|$ is $0.0008$, $0.033$ and $0.102$
against log-distances $0.10$, $1.30$ and $3.19$ between the start and
the rest point it induces — monotone in that distance, and inside one
grid atom at the start that is nearly self-consistent. A closed form
that agreed everywhere would be evidence that something had been assumed
away.

One control makes the extra term in (11) earn its place. Replacing (11)
with the worker’s envelope — deleting exactly the direct flow term
$g\zeta\varphi$ — misses the computed rate by $0.068$ to $0.091$ across
four cells, against $0.004$ to $0.033$ for the correct envelope on the
same four. The deletion is therefore visible at every cell tried and not
merely at the ones where the wedges are extreme, and it is visible in
the right direction: without capital’s own claim on his felicity, the
capitalist is predicted to reinvest too little.

At the reported cells the equilibrium corner survives initial stocks
$z_0 \in \{0.2,\,1,\,5\}$ at $\theta = 0.60$, $0.70$ and $0.74$ alike;
only at $\theta=0.78$ does the high-stock start return an interior
$\phi^\ast=0.83$. The worker meets a larger inherited stock by taxing
less — at $\theta=0.70$, $\tau^\ast$ falls from $0.48$ to $0.35$ as
$z_0$ rises from $1$ to $5$ — and the lighter tax is what sustains the
corner. Computed as a self-consistent stationary equilibrium the corner
holds for $\theta \lesssim 0.76$ rather than $\theta \lesssim 0.79$.
Nothing here is a welfare condition gap: the composite still equalises
on equilibrium, so the equalisation boundary continues to bind. What is
established is incidence — at the reported cells capital funds the
reproduction stock at the worker’s chosen maximum, and both classes gain
at the margin from its accumulation.

# 7. Stability and Distributional Cycles

Lemma 1 turns on stationarity, so the first of its three exits is to
give up the rest point altogether: if the class ratio never settles, the
indices need not equalise period by period and the wedge is unpinned.
That makes bounded distributional cycles a substantive question rather
than a footnote about dynamics. In the class in which Section 6 closes
the equilibrium, the route by which such a cycle would be born *at a
rest point* is closed everywhere in the parameter space by a bound. That
is the local route only; a far-field orbit is a separate question, taken
up at the end of the section.
