it collapses to is the result.

One remark on the workforce weight before it does, because the section
has not needed it until here. The weight reaches $\Phi'$ through the
reinvestment rate and through nothing else. Neither $\mathcal{H}$ nor
$\mathcal{P}$ carries $\omega$, by their definitions in Proposition 6;
nor does $\mathcal{U}$, whose two pole terms carry only $\alpha$,
$\theta$ and the depreciation rates and whose third is
$\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial\tau$ against
$\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast/\mathrm{d}g$ — the first free of the weight by part
(i), the second because (9) builds $\tau^\ast$ from $\Omega$, $J$ and
$\theta$ alone. Inside the rate the weight sits in two objects and no
others, $\zeta=\alpha+\omega(1-\alpha)$ and
$\mathcal{Q}=(1-\omega)+J\zeta$, and those two satisfy
$$\mathcal{Q}-J\zeta \;=\; 1-\omega,
\qquad
\mathcal{Q} \;=\; 1+\alpha J-\omega\eta, \qquad \eta=1-(1-\alpha)J\in(0,1).$$
The second display is why Proposition 5$'''$(i) is stated at $\omega=0$:
above that weight the reinvestment bracket acquires the defect
$-\omega\eta$, so $r\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)+1=N/\mathcal{M}$
is exact there and at no other weight — at $\alpha=0.33$, $\theta=0.6$,
$\gamma=0.5$, $\tilde\beta=0.9$, $J=0.75$ and $\Omega=0.53$ the two
sides are $2.074$ against $2.249$ at $\omega=0.35$. The *first* display
is why that costs the collapse nothing, and it is used three times
below. What replaces the closed form is already in the paper twice over:
Proposition 5$'''$(iii) writes $\Lambda$ at every weight, and §8.5
records the proportionality that follows,
$\Lambda\propto\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon$ with
$\Upsilon=r\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)$, against $1+\Upsilon$ at
the baseline weight. The same substitution carried Theorem 6 above
$\omega=0$. It carries this section too, and everything below holds at
every $\omega\in[0,1)$; only the subdivision at the end is run at one
weight, and that is a choice of cell rather than a step in the argument.
The interior branch needs nothing separate: it differs from the ceiling
one by the tax channel alone, and that channel carries no weight, so
what is proved below is the whole of $\Phi'$ on both branches.

Three identities do the collapse, none of them numerical, and each holds
at every weight. First,
$\tilde\beta\big[(1-\delta)+\alpha(g-1+\delta)\big]=g-D_x$ with
$\partial\tilde\beta/\partial g=-(\sigma-1)\tilde\beta/g$ gives
$$\frac{D_x'}{D_x}+\frac{\sigma-1}{g} \;=\; \frac{\sigma-\tilde\beta\alpha}{D_x},$$
positive on $\tilde\beta\alpha<1$ alone, carrying no derivative at all,
and free of the weight because $D_x$ is. Second, $R$ itself. Writing
$W=\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega$ and using
$\rho=(1-\alpha)\mathcal{Q}/\mathcal{M}$ from $(\mathrm{B}.7)$, (12)
gives $R$ outright:
$$R \;=\; \frac{\mathcal{N}_\omega}{(1-\omega)\,\mathcal{M}},
\qquad
\mathcal{N}_\omega \;=\; \zeta\mathcal{M}+\alpha\tilde\beta\gamma(1-\alpha)\,W\mathcal{Q},$$
so
$R'/R=\mathcal{N}_\omega'/\mathcal{N}_\omega-\mathcal{M}'/\mathcal{M}$
and $\rho$ and $r$ leave the expression with it. At $\omega=0$ this is
Proposition 5$'''$(i) exactly: $\mathcal{N}_0=\alpha N$ with
$N=1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma)$, and $R=\alpha N/\mathcal{M}$ again. Third,
and this is the one that does the work, Proposition 5$'''$’s own
$\mathcal{S}$ carries no weight at all, and
$$1+\mathcal{S}\;=\;\frac{\mathcal{M}_0}{\mathcal{M}},
\qquad
1+\Lambda\;=\;\big(1+\mathcal{S}\big)\,\frac{\mathcal{Q}}{1-\omega},$$
where
$\mathcal{M}_0=1-\tilde\beta+\tilde\beta\gamma\big[(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)+\alpha W\big]$
is a sum of positive terms. The first of the pair is $\mathcal{S}$’s
definition together with $\eta=1-(1-\alpha)J$; the second is
$\mathcal{Q}-J\zeta=1-\omega$ once more. At the baseline weight the two
collapse into the single statement
$\mathcal{M}(1+\Lambda)/(1+\alpha J)=\mathcal{M}_0$. What the pair buys,
with the second identity, is the rate itself in closed form,
$$i^\ast \;=\; \frac{J\zeta}{\mathcal{Q}\,\big(1-v_\omega\big)},
\qquad
v_\omega\;=\;\frac{\alpha\tilde\beta\gamma(1-\alpha)\,W\,(1-\omega)}{\mathcal{N}_\omega}\;\in\;(0,1),$$
the membership being
$\mathcal{N}_\omega-\zeta\mathcal{M}_0=\alpha\tilde\beta\gamma(1-\alpha)W(1-\omega)>0$,
which is $\mathcal{Q}-J\zeta=1-\omega$ a third time. At $\omega=0$,
where $\zeta=\alpha$ and $\mathcal{Q}=1+\alpha J$, this is
$\alpha J\big/\big[(1+\alpha J)(1-v_0)\big]$ with
$v_0=\tilde\beta\gamma(1-\alpha)W/N$, the last step using
$(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)+W=1$. Together the three leave
$$\frac{D_x'}{D_x}+\frac{\sigma-1}{g}+\frac{\Lambda'}{1+\Lambda}-\frac{R'}{R}
   \;=\;\frac{(1-\omega)\sigma+\omega\tilde\beta}{D_x\,\mathcal{Q}}
   \;-\;\frac{v_\omega'}{1-v_\omega},$$ two terms where there were four.
The first is positive by inspection, its numerator a convex combination
of $\sigma$ and $\tilde\beta$; at $\omega=0$ that numerator is $\sigma$
and its denominator $D_x(1+\alpha J)$, which is the baseline form. The
only derivative surviving anywhere in the bracket is $v_\omega'$.

That is what buys the sharpening, and the sharpening is that
$(\ddagger)$ is more than the ceiling branch needs. Divide
$\mathcal{N}_\omega$ by $\alpha\gamma(1-\alpha)\tilde\beta W(1-\omega)$
and both $J$ and $\mathcal{M}$ leave, so that every $g$ that remains
sits in one quotient, $$\frac{1}{v_\omega}
 \;=\; \frac{\zeta}{\alpha\gamma(1-\alpha)(1-\omega)}\cdot
       \frac{\tilde\beta^{-1}-1+\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)}{\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega}
 \;+\;\frac{\zeta}{(1-\alpha)(1-\omega)}\;+\;1 .$$ That quotient’s
numerator is positive — $\tilde\beta<1$ by properness and $\Omega<1$ by
Proposition 3(i) — and rises in $g$ whenever $\Omega'\le0$, since
$\partial_g\tilde\beta<0$ under Assumption 3; its denominator is
positive and falls. Two things follow at once, and neither needs a
computation. The right-hand side exceeds one, which returns
$v_\omega\in(0,1)$ by a second route that never forms $\mathcal{M}_0$;
and $\Omega'\le0$ forces $v_\omega'<0$, which makes the second term of
the bracket positive and $\Phi'>0$ outright. **Only the $\Omega$ half of
$(\ddagger)$ is used, and it is used at every weight.** $(\ddagger)$
signs $J$ and $\Omega$ together; the ceiling branch needs the
public-stock wedge alone — $J'$ does not appear in the display at all —
and the weakening is not vacuous: at $144$ of $2{,}320$ sampled points
$(\ddagger)$ fails through its $J$ half while $\Omega'\le0$ holds, and
both conditions are free of the weight, so the count is too. Where
$\Omega'>0$ the sign is not settled by this argument and the two terms
must be compared, which is a subdivision and not a sign question.

On the reduced form that subdivision runs, and it runs over both
instruments at once. It is run at $\omega=0$, the weight the reported
cells carry; by the display above it is needed only where $\Omega'>0$,
and the weight would reach it through $\zeta$ and $\mathcal{Q}$ alone —
the numerator, and $v_\omega$’s two outer constants. Take $(g,\gamma)$
in $[g_{\mathrm{fl}},8]\times[0,1]$ — the *closed* $\gamma$-box, which
settles Assumption 2’s open one a fortiori because every object above is
defined and continuous at both ends, and an upper endpoint licensed cell
by cell by $\mathcal{H}(8)>\sup\mathcal{P}$, a difference affine in
$\gamma$ and so checked at its two endpoints rather than sampled. A
completed interval cover then closes $63$ of the $216$ cells, among them
**every one of the $36$ stalled cells with $\delta_G<\delta$** and $21$
of the $45$ with $\delta<\delta_G$. At those cells, and at that weight,
Proposition 6$''$(iv)–(v) holds for every admissible $\gamma$ at once
and with no appeal to $(\ddagger)$ at all, so the outer fixed point is
unique there. No box, on any cell, returned $\Phi'\le0$.

The cells that do not close are cells on which the *enclosure* stalls,
and it is worth saying where, because the reason is again a form and not
a margin. They are concentrated at $\delta=\delta_G$ — thirty-six cells,
the balance of the $117$ after the $36$ with $\delta_G<\delta$ and the
$45$ with $\delta<\delta_G$, so the diagonal is a third case and not a
rounding of either neighbour. There the two depreciation floors
coincide, so $g-1+\delta$ and $g-1+\delta_G$ vanish *together* at the
admissible floor; $\Omega$ and $J$ vanish with them; and the denominator
$(1-\theta)\Omega+\alpha JW$ of (9) is then exactly zero, so the tax
itself is a $0/0$ there. An enclosure cannot evaluate that, and until
the shared factor is cleared — $\tau^\ast$ written as
$(1-\theta)u_G D_x/\big[(1-\theta)u_G D_x+\alpha u W D_G\big]$, whose
numerator and denominator share the factor that vanishes — the
subdivision never reaches the margin at all. Cleared, it does: at
$\alpha=0.20$, $\theta=0.30$, $\delta=\delta_G=0.02$, $\sigma=1.2$,
$\beta=0.90$ the tax has the limit $0.921$ at that floor, comfortably
above $\bar\tau$, so the ceiling branch is the live one there and the
margin is the ceiling margin. Clearing it moves six of those thirty-six
cells from stalled to closed. The rest still stall, and where they do
the remaining obstruction is $1/(g-1+\delta_G)$ at the same floor — a
divergence *upward*, with a positive coefficient, which is the direction
that settles a sign rather than the direction that obstructs one. An
enclosure that rejects it rather than using its lower bound is
discarding the easiest boxes in the subdivision, and that too is a fact
about the instrument.

One thing more, and it is about the other branch, because the interior
margin reduces as well and by the same two identities. Written as
logarithmic derivatives, both wedges are closed forms carrying no
derivative at all: the first identity above gives
$(\ln J)'=(g-1+\delta)^{-1}-(\sigma-\tilde\beta\alpha)/D_x$, and the
public column’s counterpart is (22)’s $\varepsilon_\Omega$ divided by
$g$ — an identity in $g$, as Appendix G.1 records, rather than a
consequence of standing at a zero —
$$(\ln\Omega)' \;=\; \frac{1}{g-1+\delta_G} \;-\; \frac{\sigma}{D_G}.$$
Both are one shape, and saying so is worth a line, because the shape is
$(\ddagger)$’s. The identity behind the first is the one displayed
above, $D_x+\tilde\beta\big[\alpha(g-1+\delta)+1-\delta\big]=g$; the
public column has its own, $D_G+(1-\delta_G)\tilde\beta=g$, which is
$D_G$’s definition with $g-1+\delta_G$ restored. Each turns its
logarithmic derivative into a single quotient whose numerator is a
margin: $$(\ln J)' \;=\; -\frac{M_J}{(g-1+\delta)\,D_x},
\qquad
(\ln\Omega)' \;=\; -\frac{M_\Omega}{(g-1+\delta_G)\,D_G},$$
$$M_J \;=\; (\sigma-1)(g-1+\delta)-(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta),
\qquad
M_\Omega \;=\; (\sigma-1)(g-1+\delta_G)-(1-\delta_G)(1-\tilde\beta).$$
Proposition 5$'''$(ii) already states these two margins, as the
conditions equivalent to $J'<0$ and $\Omega'<0$; what the identities add
is that they are not merely the *sign* of each wedge’s slope but its
*numerator*, over a denominator the model has already named.
$(\ddagger)$ is therefore $M_J\ge0$ and $M_\Omega\ge0$ — two conditions
affine in $(g,\tilde\beta)$ jointly — and the single condition at
$\delta_\wedge$ is the observation that the smaller depreciation rate
carries the binding one. Since $\tau^\ast$ and $1-\tau^\ast$ of (9) are
shares summing to one, $(\ln d)'$ is exactly the $\tau^\ast$-weighted
average of $(\ln\Omega)'$ and $(\ln J)'+(\ln W)'$; the two coefficients
that survive are then exact negatives of each other, and a single scalar
multiplies everything that is not the ceiling core:
$$\Phi' \;=\; \frac1g \;+\; \frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\,\mathcal{B}
   \;+\; \frac{c\,\tau^\ast}{g-1+\delta_G}
   \;+\; \Delta\left[\frac{1}{g-1+\delta}-\frac{\sigma-\tilde\beta\alpha}{D_x}
        +\frac{\sigma}{D_G}+\frac{(1-\theta)\Omega'}{W}\right],$$
$$c \;=\; \frac{\gamma\big[(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)+\alpha\big]}{1-\alpha}\;>\;0,
\qquad
\Delta \;=\; c\,\big(\tau^{\mathcal{P}}-\tau^\ast\big),$$ where
$\mathcal{B}$ denotes the two-term right-hand side of the collapse
display above, which is where the weight sits and the only place it
does. In the margins just defined the bracket is two terms rather than
four, and its poles are then visible rather than inferred:
$$\frac{1}{g-1+\delta}-\frac{\sigma-\tilde\beta\alpha}{D_x}+\frac{\sigma}{D_G}
   +\frac{(1-\theta)\Omega'}{W}
\;=\; \frac{\theta\sigma+(1-\theta)\tilde\beta}{W D_G}
   \;-\;\frac{M_J}{(g-1+\delta)\,D_x},$$ the first term strictly
positive at every admissible point and the second $-(\ln J)'$. Two
things read off it. The public column’s pole is not cancelled in the
bracket, it is **absent**: the only pole the bracket carries is the
private one at $g=1-\delta$, and $W D_G=\theta D_G+(1-\theta)
\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta_G)$ is a polynomial, so the first term needs no
$\Omega$ at all. And under $(\ddagger)$ the second term is non-positive,
so the bracket is bounded above by
$\big[\theta\sigma+(1-\theta)\tilde\beta\big]/(W D_G)$ without any
appeal to a derivative. What the bracket *is*, is an elimination.
Against part (i)’s closed form for $\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial\tau$ it
satisfies
$$\frac{1}{g-1+\delta}-\frac{\sigma-\tilde\beta\alpha}{D_x}+\frac{\sigma}{D_G}
   +\frac{(1-\theta)\Omega'}{W}
\;=\; \frac{1}{g-1+\delta_G}\;-\;
      \frac{\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast/\mathrm{d}g}{\tau^\ast\big(1-\tau^\ast\big)},$$
so $\Delta$ times it is $\gamma$ times $(13''')$’s third term plus
$\Delta/(g-1+\delta_G)$: the reduction takes
$\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast/\mathrm{d}g$ out of a term the model already had,
and that is all it does. Four things follow.

First, $\Omega'$ is the only derivative anywhere in it. $J'$ does not
appear, and it never had to: part (iii)’s
$\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast/\mathrm{d}g$ carries $J'$ only because it is formed
by the quotient rule without substituting the first identity. Second,
the multiplier is part (ii)’s own gap, so the quantity that signs the
tax channel of the loop signs the whole interior correction — and its
sign is exact and carries no derivative either, since clearing two
positive denominators leaves $$\tau^\ast \gtrless \tau^{\mathcal{P}}
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\Omega \gtrless (1-\eta)\,W ,$$ the public-stock wedge against the
workforce weight times the share of the $\ln N_W$ row that capital’s own
feedback leaves unclaimed, $(1-\alpha)J=1-\eta$ being $(\mathrm{B}.7')$.

Third, the multiplier is *bounded*, sharply and uniformly in $g$.
$\Delta=c(\tau^{\mathcal{P}}-\tau^\ast)$ is affine and strictly
decreasing in $\tau^\ast$, so $\tau^\ast\in(0,1)$ — which (9) delivers,
both wedges being positive — and nothing else gives
$$-\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\;<\;\Delta\;<\;\gamma(1-\theta),$$ both
endpoints exact and neither attained. They are not new constants. The
lower one, $c\big(\tau^{\mathcal{P}}-1\big)$, is the residue recorded
above at the $\delta<\delta_G$ endpoint, where $\tau^\ast\to1$; the
upper one is $c\,\tau^{\mathcal{P}}=\gamma(1-\theta)$, which is
$\mathcal{H}$’s own coefficient at $\delta_G$. And
$c\,\tau^\ast+\Delta=\gamma(1-\theta)$ identically, so the tax leaves
the *coefficient* of that pole — the residue is a separate question,
settled at the endpoints above — and the display is equivalently
$$\Phi' \;=\; \frac1g \;+\; \frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\,\mathcal{B}
   \;+\; \frac{\gamma(1-\theta)}{g-1+\delta_G}
   \;-\; \frac{\partial\mathcal{P}}{\partial\tau}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast}{\mathrm{d}g}.$$
Whatever is left open is a question about the tax derivative, not about
what multiplies it.

Fourth, the sign of the correction is a statement about direction of
travel rather than about level. Since $\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial\tau$
vanishes only at $\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$ and $\tau^\ast(1-\tau^\ast)>0$,
the last term above is non-negative exactly when
$$\big(\tau^\ast-\tau^{\mathcal{P}}\big)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast}{\mathrm{d}g}\;\ge\;0,$$
that is, exactly where $\tau^\ast$ is moving *away* from the
growth-maximising tax — equivalently where
$\big(\tau^\ast-\tau^{\mathcal{P}}\big)^2$ is increasing in $g$. So
$\mathcal{B}\ge0$ together with that one product non-negative already
forces $\Phi'>0$, with no appeal to $(\ddagger)$ and none to
$\mathcal{U}\ge0$: two conditions, of which the first is what
$\Omega'\le0$ delivers, and both stated on quantities the model has
already named. It is worth saying what this does *not* claim. The
condition is not weaker than requiring $\Delta\ge0$ and the bracket
non-negative separately; neither nests inside the other, since $\Delta$
times the bracket exceeds the tax channel by $\Delta/(g-1+\delta_G)$.
What is strictly weaker than that pair is the product
$\Delta\times$bracket being non-negative, which suffices on its own
alongside $\mathcal{B}\ge0$ because $c\,\tau^\ast>0$, and which admits
the case where the two factors are negative together.

What the reduction does not buy is the case that remains. At the
reported cell $\tau^\ast=0.4993$ against $\tau^{\mathcal{P}}=0.4482$, so
$\Delta<0$ there; and since the tax already sits above
$\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$, the criterion says the correction is a drag
exactly where the stationary tax is *falling* in $g$, and a boost where
it is rising. At the $\delta<\delta_G$ endpoint $\tau^\ast\to1$, so
$\Delta\to-\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)$ and the display returns the
divergence recorded above with its coefficient. The reduction relocates
the interior question onto the sign of one product, and where that sign
is adverse onto its size against the core; it does not close it.

The sign of $\Delta$ is not a cell-by-cell matter, and what decides it
is a good deal smaller than the model. Cross-multiplying (9) against
$\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$, the factors $(1-\theta)$ and $\alpha$ both cancel,
so $\tau^\ast>\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$ holds exactly when
$\Omega>(1-\alpha)JW$ — and that has a closed form. Write $u=g-1+\delta$
and $u_G=g-1+\delta_G$ for the two gross-investment margins; then
$1-\delta=g-u$ and $1-\delta_G=g-u_G$, the products $u\,u_G$ cancel
identically, $W$ is absorbed, and $$\Omega-(1-\alpha)JW \;=\;
\frac{\tilde\beta\,(1-\tilde\beta)\,g}{D_x D_G}\,\big[\,u_G-(1-\alpha)\theta\,u\,\big].$$
Every factor outside the bracket is strictly positive on the admissible
region, so the criterion *is* the bracket; and a bracket of that shape
is one threshold in the growth factor, measured from the admissibility
floor. For $\lambda\in(0,1)$,
$$u_G-\lambda u \;=\; (1-\lambda)\Big[\,g-(1-\delta_G)
   -\frac{\lambda\,(\delta-\delta_G)}{1-\lambda}\,\Big]. \tag{$\flat$}$$
Three of the conditions this section uses are brackets of that shape at
three weights, and not one of them carries $\tilde\beta$, $\sigma$,
$\gamma$ or $\omega$. At $\lambda=(1-\alpha)\theta$ the bracket signs
$\tau^\ast-\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$, and hence $\Delta$, exactly. At
$\lambda=1-\alpha$ it signs $\Omega-(1-\alpha)J$, which — since
$\eta=1-(1-\alpha)J$ — is $\Omega+\eta>1$, two quantities the model has
already named and already placed in $(0,1)$; it is sufficient for the
first and not necessary, because $W<1$. At $\lambda=\theta$ it is the
depreciation wedge $\mathcal{D}$ below. The shape is in the bracket
rather than in a general identity: $\Omega-\lambda J$ has no such closed
form at an arbitrary weight, the coefficient of $u\,u_G$ there being
$1-\tilde\beta\alpha-\lambda$, which combines with the rest only at the
weights just named.

By $(\flat)$ the order of these conditions is the sign of
$\delta-\delta_G$ and nothing else. Where $\delta\le\delta_G$ every
threshold lies at or below the floor $1-\delta_G$, so every one of them
holds at every admissible point and none of them restricts anything.
Where $\delta>\delta_G$ the thresholds rise strictly with $\lambda$, so
the conditions nest in the order of their weights. Two of the three are
ordered by that at every parameter value: $\theta<1$ puts
$(1-\alpha)\theta$ strictly below $1-\alpha$, which is why the
$\theta$-free comparison is sufficient for $\Delta<0$ and not necessary.
Where $\theta$ itself falls relative to $1-\alpha$, and so where
$\mathcal{D}$’s threshold sits among them, is not settled by the model.

At the reported cells they are a ladder — $0.9702$ at
$(1-\alpha)\theta$, $0.9950$ at $\theta$, $1.0109$ at $1-\alpha$ — and
the two computed equilibria fall inside it rather than beside it. At
$\omega=0$, $g=1.0103$ clears the first two and misses the third, so
$\Delta<0$ there while the $\theta$-free comparison fails by
$8\times10^{-4}$; at $\omega=0.50$, $g=1.0252$ clears all three. Above
the exact threshold any sufficient condition asking $\Delta\ge0$ is
vacuous, and the criterion pairing $\mathcal{B}\ge0$ with
$\big(\tau^\ast-\tau^{\mathcal{P}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast/\mathrm{d}g\ge0$
loses a factor rather than failing — the first factor is signed, so what
remains is $\mathcal{B}\ge0$ together with the stationary tax rising in
$g$. $(\ddagger)$ holds at both equilibria and separates neither, and in
these terms it is clear why. Because
$D_G=u_G+(1-\delta_G)(1-\tilde\beta)$, its public half is
$\sigma u_G>D_G$; because
$D_x=u(1-\tilde\beta\alpha)+(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)$, its capital half
is $u(\sigma-\tilde\beta\alpha)>D_x$; and since
$d\mapsto(\sigma-1)(g-1+d)-(1-d)(1-\tilde\beta)$ is increasing,
$(\ddagger)$ at $\delta_\wedge$ is exactly that pair. Both denominators
are strictly positive on the admissible region —
$\sigma>1>\tilde\beta\alpha$ — so dividing each through,
$$(\ddagger) \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad \Omega\;>\;\frac{\tilde\beta}{\sigma}
\quad\text{and}\quad J\;>\;\frac{\tilde\beta}{\sigma-\tilde\beta\alpha}.$$
$(\ddagger)$ therefore bounds each wedge below by the effective discount
factor scaled by a curvature term, which is what signing $J$ and
$\Omega$ together amounts to; $(\flat)$ compares $u_G$ against a
fraction of $u$. Neither implies the other, and both differences are
occupied: $(\ddagger)$ holds while $(\flat)$ fails at $\alpha=0.25$,
$\sigma=3$, $\delta=0.75$, $\delta_G=0.25$, $g=1.25$, $\beta=0.781$; and
$(\flat)$ holds while $(\ddagger)$ fails at $\alpha=0.5$,
$\sigma=1.125$, $\delta=\delta_G=0.5$, $g=1.5$, $\beta=0.526$.

One comparison is ruled out first, and what rules it out fixes the shape
the statement has to take. The natural move sets $\mathcal{B}$ aside and
asks whether the part of $\Phi'$ that does not carry it — that is,
$1/g+c\tau^\ast/(g-1+\delta_G)+\Delta\times$bracket — is positive on its
own, using $\mathcal{B}\ge0$ through its sign alone. It is not. There
are admissible primitives at which both halves of $(\ddagger)$ hold, the
tax is strictly interior, and that quantity is negative while $\Phi'$
itself is positive by an order of magnitude; and they are not confined
to a corner of the parameter space, appearing at capital shares from
$0.05$ to $0.95$, at curvatures down to $\sigma=1.5$, and at stationary
taxes from $0.07$ to $0.999$. The difference in every case is
$\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)$ times $\mathcal{B}$, and that coefficient is
the one that bounds $\Delta$ below. Setting $\mathcal{B}$ aside
therefore discards the term that carries the result, and the correction
and the core have to enter one statement rather than three.

They can, at no bound beyond the two already displayed. Both ends of
$\Delta$’s range are exact and both match, in magnitude, a coefficient
$\Phi'$ already carries — the lower one $\mathcal{B}$’s, the upper one
the public pole’s — so each set against its own term, the constant that
looked unbounded cancels. Two steps make that precise: the first writes
the bracket as the public pole less a single scalar, and the second
observes that the scalar, like $\mathcal{B}$, does not move with the
tax.

**Proposition 6$'''$ (the growth channel is affine in the stationary
tax).** *Let the hypotheses of Proposition 6$''$ hold in full:
Assumptions 1–5 with $\theta\in(0,1)$ and $\omega<1$, at $\phi=1$ with
$i$ and the tax interior, and at a $g$ where the constrained tax
$\min\{\tau^\ast,\bar\tau\}$ is differentiable — the point-hypothesis
Proposition 6$'$ carries, and the only one used below beyond the
identities. Write $\mathcal{O}$ for the scalar*
$$\mathcal{O} \;=\; \frac{M_J}{(g-1+\delta)\,D_x}\;-\;\frac{\theta}{W}\,
   \frac{M_\Omega}{(g-1+\delta_G)\,D_G}$$ *and $\mathcal{X}$ for the
part of $\Phi'$ that carries no tax wedge,*
$$\mathcal{X} \;=\; \frac1g \;+\; \frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\,\mathcal{B}
   \;+\; \frac{\gamma(1-\theta)}{g-1+\delta_G} .$$ *Then:*

*(i) **The bracket is the public pole less one logarithmic derivative.**
The bracket above equals $1/(g-1+\delta_G)-\mathcal{O}$, and*
$$\mathcal{O} \;=\; -(\ln J)' \;+\; \frac{\theta}{W}\,(\ln\Omega)'
\;=\; \Big(\ln\frac{\tau^\ast}{1-\tau^\ast}\Big)' :$$ *the scalar is the
rate at which the odds on the stationary tax move with the growth
factor. This regroups the bracket; it is not a second derivative.*

*(ii) **The channel is affine in the tax.** Neither $\mathcal{B}$ nor
$\mathcal{O}$ depends on $\tau^\ast$, and
$\Delta=c(\tau^{\mathcal{P}}-\tau^\ast)$ is affine in it, so*
$$\Phi' \;=\; \mathcal{X}\;-\;\Delta\,\mathcal{O},
\qquad \mathcal{X}\;>\;0 \ \text{ under the } \Omega \text{ half of } (\ddagger).$$
*The affine map
$t\mapsto\mathcal{X}-c(\tau^{\mathcal{P}}-t)\,\mathcal{O}$ on $[0,1]$
agrees with $\Phi'$ at $t=\tau^\ast$, and its multiplier takes the value
$\gamma(1-\theta)$ at $t=0$ and $-\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)$ at $t=1$ —
the two ends of $\Delta$’s sharp range, matching in magnitude the
coefficients $\mathcal{X}$ carries on the public pole and on
$\mathcal{B}$ respectively.*

*(iii) **Three endpoint bounds, exhausting the sign pairs.***
$$\Delta\mathcal{O}\le0:\ \ \Phi'\ge\mathcal{X};
\qquad
\Delta<0,\ \mathcal{O}<0:\ \ \Phi'>\mathcal{X}+\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\,\mathcal{O};
\qquad
\Delta>0,\ \mathcal{O}>0:\ \ \Phi'>\mathcal{X}-\gamma(1-\theta)\,\mathcal{O}.$$
*Which endpoint the last two use is settled with no derivative at all:
it is $t=1$ when $\Delta<0$ and $t=0$ when $\Delta>0$, and $\Delta\le0$
exactly when $\Omega\ge(1-\eta)W$.*

*(iv) **They contain both standing sufficient conditions, and
strictly.** Each of
$(\tau^\ast-\tau^{\mathcal{P}})\,\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast/\mathrm{d}g\ge0$
and $\Delta\times\text{bracket}\ge0$ implies one of the three lines. And
every point with $\Delta<0$ and $\mathcal{O}<0$ fails both, while the
second line settles it whenever
$\mathcal{X}+\gamma\alpha\mathcal{O}/(1-\alpha)\ge0$.*

In words, the growth channel is a part that does not see the tax at all,
plus a correction equal to how far the tax sits above the
growth-maximising rate multiplied by the rate at which the odds on the
tax rise with growth. The second factor does not itself move with the
tax, so the channel is a straight line in it; and a straight line on an
interval is smallest at one of the two ends. Which end is decided by
whether the worker taxes above or below the growth-maximising rate,
which is the public wedge against the workforce weight and carries no
derivative. The question the branch leaves open is therefore the sign of
one number at one end, not the correlation of two quantities that both
move.

*Proof.* Every denominator below is positive on the stated domain, and
none of it is assumed: Proposition 3(i) puts $\Omega$ in $(0,1)$ exactly
under properness, so $D_G=\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta_G)/\Omega>0$ and
$W=\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\in(\theta,1)$; $D_x>0$ is Proposition 4(iii);
and $g-1+\delta>0$, $g-1+\delta_G>0$ on Proposition 6(ii)’s domain.
Assumptions 1–4 alone would not deliver even $\Omega>0$, which is why
Assumption 5 is in the hypotheses rather than in the proof.

**(i)** With $W=\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega$ and
$\Omega D_G=\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta_G)$,
$$\big[\theta\sigma+(1-\theta)\tilde\beta\big](g-1+\delta_G)
\;=\; W D_G \;+\; \theta M_\Omega ,$$ which is one line from
$M_\Omega=\sigma(g-1+\delta_G)-D_G$ and is verified in Appendix B.10.
Dividing by $WD_G(g-1+\delta_G)$ turns the two-term bracket’s first term
into $1/(g-1+\delta_G)+(\theta/W)M_\Omega/[(g-1+\delta_G)D_G]$, and its
second term is $-M_J/[(g-1+\delta)D_x]$; that is the first claim. The
two logarithmic-derivative forms are the margin displays above. For the
third, (9) at $\phi=1$ gives the odds outright,
$\tau^\ast/(1-\tau^\ast)=(1-\theta)\Omega/(J\alpha W)$, so that
$\big(\ln[\tau^\ast/(1-\tau^\ast)]\big)'=(\ln\Omega)'-(\ln J)'-(\ln W)'$;
and since $W'=(1-\theta)\Omega'$ while $W-(1-\theta)\Omega=\theta$, the
first and third of those collect into $(\theta/W)(\ln\Omega)'$. The
weight is therefore not a device but an elasticity: $\theta$ is the part
of $W$ that carries no $\Omega$, and differentiating the odds in the
public wedge alone — which enters both directly and through $W$ — gives
$\partial\ln[\tau^\ast/(1-\tau^\ast)]/\partial\ln\Omega=\theta/W$
exactly.

**(ii)** Substituting (i) into the display of $\Phi'$ collects the two
public-pole terms into $(c\tau^\ast+\Delta)/(g-1+\delta_G)$, and
$c\tau^\ast+\Delta=c\tau^{\mathcal{P}}=\gamma(1-\theta)$ identically,
which leaves $\Phi'=\mathcal{X}-\Delta\mathcal{O}$. Both $\mathcal{B}$
and $\mathcal{O}$ are functions of $g$, $\tilde\beta$ and the
primitives, so $\Delta$ is the only place $\tau^\ast$ enters; and
$\mathcal{X}>0$ because $\mathcal{B}\ge0$ under the $\Omega$ half while
$g$ and $g-1+\delta_G$ are positive throughout Proposition 6(ii)’s
domain. At $t=0$ the multiplier is
$c\tau^{\mathcal{P}}=\gamma(1-\theta)$, and at $t=1$ it is
$c\tau^{\mathcal{P}}-c=-\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)$.

**(iii)** The first line is immediate from (ii). Otherwise the affine
map has slope $c\,\mathcal{O}$ in $t$, decreasing when $\mathcal{O}<0$
and increasing when $\mathcal{O}>0$; since (9) puts $\tau^\ast$ in the
*open* interval, its value there strictly exceeds the value at $t=1$ in
the first case and at $t=0$ in the second, and those two values are the
displayed right-hand sides. The remaining sign pairs,
$\Delta<0<\mathcal{O}$ and $\mathcal{O}<0<\Delta$, give
$\Delta\mathcal{O}<0$ and fall under the first line.

**(iv)** By (i),
$\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast/\mathrm{d}g=\tau^\ast(1-\tau^\ast)\mathcal{O}$, and
$\tau^\ast-\tau^{\mathcal{P}}=-\Delta/c$ with $c>0$, so the first
standing condition *is* $\Delta\mathcal{O}\le0$. For the second, suppose
$\Delta\times$bracket$\,\ge0$. If $\Delta<0$ the bracket is
non-positive, so $\mathcal{O}\ge1/(g-1+\delta_G)>0$ and
$\Delta\mathcal{O}<0$ again. If $\Delta>0$ the bracket is non-negative,
so $\mathcal{O}\le1/(g-1+\delta_G)$: either $\mathcal{O}\le0$, which is
the first line, or the third line applies and its right-hand side is at
least
$\mathcal{X}-\gamma(1-\theta)/(g-1+\delta_G)=1/g+\gamma\alpha\mathcal{B}/(1-\alpha)>0$
— the public pole cancelling against its own coefficient. Finally, at a
point with $\Delta<0$ and $\mathcal{O}<0$ the first condition fails
because $\Delta\mathcal{O}>0$, and the second fails because the bracket
then exceeds $1/(g-1+\delta_G)>0$ while $\Delta<0$; the second line
settles such a point whenever its right-hand side is non-negative.
$\blacksquare$

Three things follow, and the first is what the reduction is worth. The
correction to the growth channel is the tax’s distance from its
growth-maximising level, multiplied by the rate at which the odds on
that tax move with growth — and nothing else. The two standing
conditions ask a product of two moving quantities to keep a sign; part
(iii) asks a single number at one endpoint to keep one, and the
equivalence
$\tau^\ast\gtrless\tau^{\mathcal{P}}\Leftrightarrow\Omega\gtrless(1-\eta)W$
above says which endpoint without differentiating anything. That is the
whole gain, and it is a gain in coordinates rather than in strength: no
bound on $\Delta$ is sharper here than the one already displayed.

Second, the direction in which the relaxation runs. Reading $\tau^\ast$
as free is a relaxation, since (9) pins it, so sufficiency travels and
refutation does not: a $t$ at which an endpoint value is negative is not
a point of the model and refutes nothing. Nor is any of it stated at one
weight. $\mathcal{B}$ enters exactly as it enters $\Phi'$, and
$\mathcal{O}$, $\Delta$, $c$ and $\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$ carry no $\omega$
by parts (i)–(ii). Dropping $\Delta$’s sign altogether gives one
inequality with no case in it at all, weaker than the three lines but
free of the branch: $\Phi'>0$ whenever
$\gamma(1-\theta)\mathcal{O}^{+}+\gamma\alpha\mathcal{O}^{-}/(1-\alpha)<\mathcal{X}$,
the superscripts being positive and negative parts. Only the $\Omega$
half of $(\ddagger)$ is used in any of this, and it enters through
$\mathcal{X}>0$ alone, the two endpoint values being numbers.

Third, what is still open, and how far the numbers reach. On a lattice
of $243{,}756$ admissible points satisfying both halves of $(\ddagger)$
the three lines close every one and the two standing conditions close
$214{,}008$; $29{,}748$ points therefore lie in the difference. That is
a census and settles no point it did not visit. What $(\ddagger)$ alone
does not deliver — off the band Proposition 6$''''$ below identifies, on
which it does — is the sign of the second and third lines’ right-hand
sides, and that is now a scalar at one endpoint rather than a
correlation between two quantities that both move.

On the falling side there is a sufficient condition cheaper than the
endpoint value itself, and it is about depreciation rather than about
preferences. There $\Phi'>0$ follows from $\mathcal{B}\ge|\mathcal{O}|$;
$M_J\ge0$ makes
$|\mathcal{O}|\le(\theta/W)M_\Omega/[(g-1+\delta_G)D_G]$, and discarding
$\mathcal{B}$’s derivative term — non-negative under the $\Omega$ half,
by the $1/v_\omega$ display above — leaves a comparison that clears
completely. Writing
$\widehat D=D_x(1+\alpha J)=g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta)$, which is
$\mathcal{B}$’s own denominator at $\omega=0$ and is affine in
$\tilde\beta$, and $\mathcal{D}$ for the depreciation wedge
$WD_G-\theta\widehat D=\tilde\beta\mathcal{D}$,
$$\frac{\sigma}{\widehat D}-\frac{\theta}{W}\,\frac{M_\Omega}{(g-1+\delta_G)D_G}
\;=\;\frac{\sigma\tilde\beta\,\mathcal{D}\,(g-1+\delta_G)+\theta\,\widehat D\,D_G}
          {W\,\widehat D\,D_G\,(g-1+\delta_G)},
\qquad \mathcal{D}=(1-\theta)(g-1+\delta_G)+\theta(\delta_G-\delta)=u_G-\theta u,$$
a numerator that is a sum of non-negative terms whenever
$\mathcal{D}\ge0$. Written that second way the condition is
$u_G\ge\theta u$, which is $(\flat)$’s bracket at $\lambda=\theta$. By
$(\flat)$, $$\mathcal{D}\ge0 \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
g\;\ge\;(1-\delta_G)+\frac{\theta\,(\delta-\delta_G)}{1-\theta},$$
automatic at $\delta\le\delta_G$, where the threshold falls to the
admissibility floor or below, and a floor on $g$ strictly above it
otherwise. The condition carries no $\gamma$, no $\alpha$, no $\sigma$
and no $\tilde\beta$: it is about the two depreciation rates, the
workforce weight and the growth factor, and nothing else. The reported
cells have $\delta>\delta_G$, so $\mathcal{D}$ turns negative at the
higher $\theta$; the numerator stays positive there on its second term,
which is what that term is kept for. Those cells do not need it in any
case — at their primitives the first line of part (iii) applies at every
$g$ from the admissibility floor to $8$, the stationary tax rising in
$g$ while it sits above $\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$. The falling side is
reached elsewhere in the parameter space, and that is where the
comparison is for.

Both conditions so far are about the two depreciation rates, and neither
is about preferences. There is one of those as well, and it does more:
it closes the branch outright, on a band of primitives, with no case on
the sign of anything, and it drops Proposition 6$''$’s second hypothesis
while doing it. What makes it available is a coincidence of poles.
$\mathcal{O}$’s two terms carry exactly the two poles $\mathcal{X}$
carries, and $\Delta$’s two sharp endpoints are exactly the two
coefficients $\mathcal{X}$ carries on them; so the correction can be met
term by term rather than as a whole.

**Proposition 6$''''$ (a band of primitives on which the interior branch
closes under $(\ddagger)$ alone).** *Let the hypotheses of Proposition
6$'''$ hold, and let $(\ddagger)$ hold. Then:*

*(i) **The correction meets the core pole by pole.** Each margin over
its own denominator is the slack in one half of $(\ddagger)$,*
$$\frac{M_J}{D_x} \;=\; \frac{(\sigma-\tilde\beta\alpha)J}{\tilde\beta}-1,
\qquad
\frac{M_\Omega}{D_G} \;=\; \frac{\sigma\Omega}{\tilde\beta}-1,$$ *both
non-negative exactly under $(\ddagger)$; and* $$\Phi' \;=\; \frac1g
 \;+\;\left[\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\,\mathcal{B}
      \;-\;\frac{\Delta}{g-1+\delta}\,\frac{M_J}{D_x}\right]
 \;+\;\frac{1}{g-1+\delta_G}\left[\gamma(1-\theta)
      \;+\;\Delta\,\frac{\theta}{W}\,\frac{M_\Omega}{D_G}\right].$$
*Each bracket sets one term of the correction against the term of the
core with the same pole; in the second the pole cancels outright.*

*(ii) **Both brackets are non-negative on a band of primitives.** If*
$$\sigma-1 \;\le\; \min\left\{\frac{(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)}{\alpha},\ \frac{\alpha}{1-\theta}\right\},
\tag{$\sharp$}$$ *then $\Phi'\ge1/g>0$ at every admissible $g$, for
every $\gamma$, every $\omega\in[0,1)$ and every $\beta$, $\delta$ and
$\delta_G$ — with no case on the sign of $\Delta$ or of $\mathcal{O}$.*

*(iii) **Both conclusions of Proposition 6$''$ then rest on $(\ddagger)$
and nothing else.** Under $(\sharp)$, parts (iv) and (v) hold without
$\mathcal{U}\ge0$. Since $\Phi'=\mathcal{H}'-\mathcal{P}_g$ and
$\mathcal{H}'>0$, $\Phi'>0$ is $\mathcal{L}'<1$, so the loop gain is
below one at every fixed point; and $\Phi$ is strictly increasing on the
whole admissible interval, so the outer fixed point is unique, at every
admissible $\gamma$.*

*(iv) **The band is non-empty at every capital share.** The supremum of
the right-hand side of $(\sharp)$ over $\theta$ is
$\min\big\{\sqrt{1-\alpha},\,(1-\alpha)/\alpha\big\}>0$, and which of
the two it is is settled by $\alpha$ alone: where $\alpha^{2}<1-\alpha$
the crossing $1-\theta=\alpha/\sqrt{1-\alpha}$ is interior and the
supremum is attained there, at $\sqrt{1-\alpha}$; where
$\alpha^{2}\ge1-\alpha$ the right-hand side rises throughout and
approaches $(1-\alpha)/\alpha$ as $\theta\to0$ without attaining it,
$\theta=0$ lying outside the domain. Since $\sigma>1$ is the only
restriction the curvature carries, non-emptiness at every $\alpha$ needs
the supremum and not its attainment.*

*(v) **Only one bracket is ever at risk, and $(\sharp)$ is the price of
not looking at which.** $\Phi'$ is affine in $\Delta$ as a whole, with
slope $-\mathcal{O}$, so it is smallest at
$\Delta=-\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)$ when $\mathcal{O}\le0$ and at
$\Delta=\gamma(1-\theta)$ when $\mathcal{O}\ge0$ — and at each of those
ends the other bracket is at its largest. Under $(\ddagger)$, therefore,
$\Phi'\ge1/g$ whenever*
$$\mathcal{O}\le0 \ \text{ and }\ \alpha(\sigma-1)\le(1-\alpha)(1-\theta),
\qquad\text{or}\qquad
\mathcal{O}\ge0 \ \text{ and }\ (1-\theta)(\sigma-1)\le\alpha :$$ *one
half of $(\sharp)$ at each point, the sign of $\mathcal{O}$ selecting
which, and $\mathcal{O}$ carrying no derivative. That region strictly
contains the band.*

*Proof.* $D_G=(g-1+\delta_G)+(1-\delta_G)(1-\tilde\beta)$ and
$D_x=(g-1+\delta)(1-\tilde\beta\alpha)+(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)$
rearrange to $M_\Omega=\sigma(g-1+\delta_G)-D_G$ and
$M_J=(g-1+\delta)(\sigma-\tilde\beta\alpha)-D_x$; dividing by $D_G$ and
by $D_x$ and using $\Omega=\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta_G)/D_G$ and
$J=\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta)/D_x$ gives the two displayed forms, whose
non-negativity is $(\ddagger)$ in the wedge-bound form above.
Substituting them into Proposition 6$'''$(i)’s $\mathcal{O}$ and then
into its part (ii) gives the second display: it is a regrouping, and the
only step in it is that $\mathcal{O}$’s public term and $\mathcal{X}$’s
public term carry the same pole $1/(g-1+\delta_G)$.

**(ii)** Each bracket is affine in $\Delta$, so it is enough to check it
at whichever end of $\Delta$’s sharp range makes it smallest. The second
is smallest at $\Delta=-\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)$, where non-negativity
is $\big(\alpha/(1-\alpha)\big)(\theta/W)M_\Omega/D_G\le1-\theta$; and
$W>\theta$ gives $\theta/W<1$ while
$D_G-(g-1+\delta_G)=(1-\delta_G)(1-\tilde\beta)\ge0$ gives
$\Omega\le\tilde\beta$ and so $M_\Omega/D_G\le\sigma-1$, which puts the
left-hand side below $\alpha(\sigma-1)/(1-\alpha)$ — and $(\sharp)$’s
first half is exactly that below $1-\theta$. The first bracket is
smallest at $\Delta=\gamma(1-\theta)$, where non-negativity is
$\big(\alpha/(1-\alpha)\big)\mathcal{B}\ge(1-\theta)M_J/\big[(g-1+\delta)D_x\big]$.
Under $(\ddagger)$ the public wedge falls, so $\mathcal{B}$’s derivative
term is non-negative and $\mathcal{B}$ is at least the collapse
display’s first term,
$\big[(1-\omega)\sigma+\omega\tilde\beta\big]/(D_x\mathcal{Q})$. That
term is linear-fractional in $\omega$ with a positive denominator, hence
monotone, hence at least its value at one of the two ends; and since
$\mathcal{Q}=(1-\omega)+J\zeta$, those values are $\sigma/\widehat D$ at
$\omega=0$ and $1/(g-1+\delta)$ at $\omega=1$. With
$M_J\le(\sigma-1)(g-1+\delta)$, the requirement at the second is
$\alpha D_x\ge(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)(\sigma-1)(g-1+\delta)$, which
$D_x\ge(g-1+\delta)(1-\tilde\beta\alpha)>(g-1+\delta)(1-\alpha)$ reduces
to $(\sharp)$’s second half; at the first,
$1+\alpha J\le1/(1-\tilde\beta\alpha)$ gives
$\sigma D_x/\widehat D>\sigma(1-\alpha)$ and the same reduction, with
$\sigma>1$ leaving it slack.

**(iii)** Where the ceiling binds, Proposition 6$''$(v)’s own argument
gives $\Phi'>\mathcal{H}'>0$ under $(\ddagger)$ alone; where the tax is
interior, (ii) gives $\Phi'\ge1/g$; and $\Phi$ is continuous across the
switch, so it is strictly increasing on the whole interval and vanishes
at most once. **(iv)** The first argument of the minimum rises in
$1-\theta$ and the second falls, and $1-\theta$ ranges over the *open*
interval $(0,1)$, $\theta=0$ being excluded by the hypotheses. Their
crossing is at $1-\theta=\alpha/\sqrt{1-\alpha}$, which lies in $(0,1)$
exactly when $\alpha^{2}<1-\alpha$; there the supremum is attained and
equals $\sqrt{1-\alpha}$. When $\alpha^{2}\ge1-\alpha$ the crossing is
at or beyond the excluded end, so the first argument binds throughout —
$\alpha^{2}-(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)^{2}>0$ for every admissible $\theta$ —
and the right-hand side is $(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)/\alpha$, strictly
increasing, with
$(1-\alpha)/\alpha-(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)/\alpha=(1-\alpha)\theta/\alpha>0$:
the supremum $(1-\alpha)/\alpha$ is approached and never attained. In
both branches the supremum is $\sqrt{1-\alpha}$ or $(1-\alpha)/\alpha$,
and in each case it is the smaller of the pair, which is the display in
the statement. **(v)** $\Phi'=\mathcal{X}-\Delta\mathcal{O}$ is affine
in $\Delta$ with slope $-\mathcal{O}$, so on $\Delta$’s range it is
smallest at the stated end; the two endpoint values are part (i)’s
display at $\Delta=\mp$ the two bounds, which are Proposition
6$'''$(iii)’s second and third lines rewritten. At
$\Delta=-\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)$ the first bracket is
$\gamma\alpha\big[\mathcal{B}+(M_J/D_x)/(g-1+\delta)\big]/(1-\alpha)$,
non-negative under $(\ddagger)$ by (ii)’s first two facts, so only the
second can fail and (ii)’s first half settles it; at
$\Delta=\gamma(1-\theta)$ the second bracket is
$\gamma(1-\theta)\big[1+(\theta/W)M_\Omega/D_G\big]/(g-1+\delta_G)>0$,
so only the first can fail and (ii)’s second half settles it.
$\blacksquare$

What $(\sharp)$ says is that each of the two things the correction can
do is capped by a term the core already carries, and that both caps are
primitive. On the public side the wedge slack is capped by the curvature
$\sigma-1$ and meets the public pole’s own coefficient
$\gamma(1-\theta)$, so the pole cancels and what is left compares
$\alpha(\sigma-1)$ with $(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)$. On the capital side the
slack meets the reinvestment term, whose scale is the private pole, and
what is left compares $(1-\theta)(\sigma-1)$ with $\alpha$. Neither
comparison carries the growth factor, the discount factor, either
depreciation rate, the reproduction elasticity or the workforce weight —
which is why the band can be stated before any $g$ is chosen. The two
run in opposite directions in $\alpha$, so $(\sharp)$ is a band and not
a half-space, and it is widest where the two bind together.

The band is not a restatement of the first line of Proposition
6$'''$(iii), because both of the sign pairs that line does not reach are
occupied inside it. At $\alpha=0.20$, $\theta=0.65$, $\sigma=1.4$,
$\beta=0.4$, $\delta=0.8$, $\delta_G=1$ and $g=1.5$ the stationary tax
sits above $\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$ and is falling; at $\alpha=0.25$,
$\theta=0.65$, $\sigma=1.5$, $\beta=0.9$, $\delta=0.85$, $\delta_G=0.2$
and $g=1.05$ it sits below and is rising. $(\ddagger)$ holds at both.
Nor does the band cover the reported cells, where
$\alpha(\sigma-1)=0.33$ against $(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)=0.268$; those are
settled by that first line instead, at every $g$ from the admissibility
floor to $8$. What the band buys is elsewhere, and it is the subdivision
itself: on it neither $\mathcal{U}$ nor an interval cover is needed, and
the growth channel is signed by four primitives.

Part (v) says what the band costs. Since $\Phi'$ is affine in $\Delta$
as a whole, its two brackets are worst at *opposite* ends of $\Delta$’s
range, and only the one at the selected end can fail; requiring both at
once is therefore conservative, and the sign of $\mathcal{O}$ — a closed
form carrying no derivative — says which is needed. The gain is not
marginal. At $\alpha=0.10$, $\theta=0.70$, $\sigma=3$, $\beta=0.7$,
$\delta=0.5$, $\delta_G=0.9$ and $g=2$ the first half holds, the second
fails, and $\mathcal{O}<0$; at $\alpha=0.20$, $\theta=0.90$, $\sigma=3$,
$\beta=0.9$, $\delta=\delta_G=0.9$ and $g=2$ the second holds, the first
fails, and $\mathcal{O}>0$. $(\ddagger)$ holds at both, and both sit at
a curvature $(\sharp)$ excludes at every $\theta$.

That the loss sits there and not in the two bounds is worth one line,
because it says where a sharper statement would have to come from.
Before their reduction the brackets ask
$$(1-\theta)\;\ge\;\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\,\frac{\theta}{W}\,\frac{M_\Omega}{D_G}
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\,\mathcal{B}\;\ge\;\frac{1-\theta}{g-1+\delta}\,\frac{M_J}{D_x},$$
and each becomes a half of $(\sharp)$ under one bound. On the public
side $M_\Omega/D_G\le\sigma-1$ and $\theta/W<1$, and both are approached
together as $\delta_G\to1$ with $\tilde\beta\to0$; on the capital side
$M_J/D_x<(\sigma-1)/(1-\alpha)$, approached as $\delta\to1$ with
$\tilde\beta\to1$. So no bound free of $\tilde\beta$ and $g$ improves
either half. But the two limits sit at opposite corners — one wants the
effective discount factor to vanish and the other wants it to reach one
— so the two halves are never tight together, which is part (v) arrived
at a second way.

One asymmetry between the branches survives all of this, and it earns a
line because the ceiling branch above uses the public half of
$(\ddagger)$ alone. The interior branch uses both, and what the capital
half buys is the absence of a pole: where $M_J<0$ the first bracket
carries $-\Delta M_J/\big[D_x(g-1+\delta)\big]$, which at $\Delta<0$ is
negative and unbounded as $g$ falls to $1-\delta$. At $\alpha=0.20$,
$\theta=0.50$, $\sigma=1.2$, $\beta=0.9$, $\delta=0.02$, $\delta_G=0.9$
and $g=1.0001$ the public half holds, the first half of $(\sharp)$ holds
with it, and part (v)’s endpoint value is $-2.57$; at the same
primitives with $g=1.2$ the capital half still fails while that value is
$+1.32$, so what defeats the first point is its proximity to the private
floor and not the sign of $M_J$. Both are statements about the bound
rather than about $\Phi'$, which the endpoints only relax. What they
establish is that the capital half is load-bearing on this branch in the
way it is not on the other.

So the interior branch leaves one scalar to sign, at an endpoint the
sign of $\Delta$ selects with no derivative in it. Which half of
$(\sharp)$ signs it is settled by the sign of $\mathcal{O}$, also with
no derivative; where the matching half holds, the branch closes under
$(\ddagger)$ by itself, and where it does not, that scalar is the whole
of what $(\ddagger)$ does not deliver here.

## 8.2 The tax margin, and the ceiling

**Proposition 7 (the tax margin single-crosses at every split, and the
ceiling is respected).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with
$\theta\in(0,1)$, let the profile be proper, and hold $g$ — hence
$\Omega$ and $J$ — fixed. Define on $\tau\in(0,1)$ the tax margin*
$$\Xi(\tau;\phi) \;=\; \frac{\theta(1-\phi)}{\kappa_W(\phi)} \;+\; \frac{(1-\theta)\Omega}{\tau\alpha}
   \;-\; \frac{J\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]}{1-\tau}, \tag{14}$$
*whose zero is (8) and whose sign is the sign of
$\partial V_W/\partial\tau$ by Lemma B.1. Then parts (i) and (ii) hold
for every $\phi\in[0,1]$, being algebra in $\Xi$ at fixed
$(g,\Omega,J)$, and part (iii) for every $\phi\in(0,1]$ — at $\phi=0$
the public stock has no source term, Proposition 2(iii) puts any
stationary $z>0$ at $g_W=1-\delta_G$ and so outside criterion (A), there
is no interior rest point, and Lemma B.1 has no marginal value to sign:*

*(i) **One crossing, from above.** $\Xi$ is strictly decreasing in
$\tau$,*
$$\frac{\partial\Xi}{\partial\tau} \;=\; -\frac{\theta(1-\phi)^{2}\alpha}{\kappa_W(\phi)^{2}}
   \;-\; \frac{(1-\theta)\Omega}{\alpha\tau^{2}}
   \;-\; \frac{J\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]}{(1-\tau)^{2}} \;<\; 0,$$
*with $\Xi\to+\infty$ as $\tau\to0^{+}$ and $\Xi\to-\infty$ as
$\tau\to1^{-}$. Hence $\Xi$ has exactly one zero
$\tau^\dagger(\phi)\in(0,1)$.*

*(ii) **The corner root is the lowest.** $\Xi$ is strictly decreasing in
$\phi$, so $\tau^\dagger$ is decreasing in $\phi$ and
$\tau^\dagger(\phi)\ge\tau^\dagger(1)$, the latter being the closed form
(9).*

*(iii) **The constrained tax respects the ceiling by construction.** The
maximiser of the worker’s one-shot objective over $[0,\bar\tau]$ is
$\min\{\tau^\dagger(\phi),\ \bar\tau\}$, which lies in $(0,\bar\tau]$
whatever the primitives.*

*Proof.* **(i)** $\kappa_W(\phi)=(1-\alpha)+(1-\phi)\tau\alpha$ has
$\partial\kappa_W/\partial\tau = (1-\phi)\alpha\ge0$, so the first term
of (14) has derivative $-\theta(1-\phi)^{2}\alpha/\kappa_W^{2}\le0$; the
second has derivative $-(1-\theta)\Omega/(\alpha\tau^{2})<0$, strictly
because $\Omega>0$ by Proposition 3(i) under properness; the third has
derivative $-J[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega]/(1-\tau)^{2}<0$, strictly
because $J>0$ by Proposition 4(iii). As $\tau\to0^{+}$ the second term
diverges to $+\infty$ while the first tends to
$\theta(1-\phi)/(1-\alpha)$ and the third to a finite negative number;
as $\tau\to1^{-}$ the third diverges to $-\infty$ while the other two
stay bounded, $\kappa_W$ being bounded below by $1-\alpha$. Strict
monotonicity with those two limits gives exactly one zero and an
up-to-down crossing.

**(ii)** Only the first term carries $\phi$, and
$\partial\big[\theta(1-\phi)/\kappa_W\big]/\partial\phi = -\theta(1-\alpha)/\kappa_W^{2}<0$,
the $(1-\phi)\tau\alpha$ terms cancelling. So
$\Xi(\tau;\phi)-\Xi(\tau;1) = \theta(1-\phi)/\kappa_W\ge0$, with
equality only at $\phi=1$. Evaluating at $\tau^\dagger(1)$ gives
$\Xi(\tau^\dagger(1);\phi)\ge0$, and strict decrease in $\tau$ then puts
$\tau^\dagger(\phi)$ weakly to the right. At $\phi=1$ the first term of
(14) vanishes, the condition is linear in $\tau$, and Proposition 4(iii)
solves it as (9).

**(iii)** The derivation of (8) collects the marginal value as
$\partial V_W/\partial\tau = \tilde\beta\gamma\alpha\,a_{N_W}\,\Xi(\tau;\phi)$,
and $a_{N_W}>0$ by Lemma B.1, so the marginal value carries the sign of
$\Xi$ and the one-shot objective at fixed $g$ rises strictly on
$(0,\tau^\dagger)$ and falls strictly on $(\tau^\dagger,1)$. Its
maximiser over $[0,\bar\tau]$ is therefore $\tau^\dagger$ where that
lies below the ceiling and $\bar\tau$ where it does not, which is the
stated truncation; it is strictly positive because both $\tau^\dagger$
and $\bar\tau$ are. $\blacksquare$

Proposition 4(iii) obtained uniqueness at the corner $\phi=1$ by
observing that the condition is linear in $\tau$ there. Away from the
corner it is not linear, and single-crossing has to be proved rather
than solved. Part (i) proves it by the route Propositions 3(ii) and 5
already use — a decreasing function minus an increasing one — so all
three instruments now have one argument between them, and in each case
the crossing is a fact about where the instrument enters rather than a
curvature hypothesis. Part (iii) settles the ceiling. That nothing in
Assumptions 1–5 forces $\tau^\dagger\le\bar\tau$ remains true and is no
longer an obstruction: the unconstrained root is not the policy, because
the worker chooses from a compact interval, and on a single-crossing
margin the constrained choice is the truncation of the root.

## 8.3 A stationary profile exists

Write $\phi^\dagger(\tau;g)$ for the root (7) and $\tau^\dagger(\phi;g)$
for the root of Proposition 7, both evaluated at the wedges $\Omega(g)$
and $J(g)$, and $\Lambda(g)$ for (12)’s bracket. The **stationary map**
sends a profile to the three constrained one-shot maximisers taken at
the rest point that profile induces:
$$\Gamma(\tau,\phi,i) \;=\; \Big(\ \min\{\bar\tau,\ \tau^\dagger(\phi;g)\},\ \
   \min\{1,\ \phi^\dagger(\tau;g)\},\ \ \frac{\Lambda(g)}{1+\Lambda(g)}\ \Big),
\qquad g \;=\; \mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{P}). \tag{15}$$ The convention
at $\tau=0$ is $\phi^\dagger=+\infty$, which is the limit of (7) there
and makes the second coordinate identically one on that face. A fixed
point of $\Gamma$ is a profile at which each instrument satisfies its
own stationary condition, or sits at the boundary the condition pushes
it to, given the other two and given the growth factor the three of them
generate. Call it a **stationary profile**.

**Theorem 3 (a stationary profile exists).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold
with $\theta\in(0,1)$ and $\bar\tau<1$. Suppose there is a number*
$$g_\star \;>\; \max\big\{1-\delta,\ 1-\delta_G,\ \beta^{1/(\sigma-1)}\big\}$$
*at which, writing
$\tau_\star=\min\{\bar\tau,\tau^\dagger(1;g_\star)\}$,
$\phi_\star=\min\{1,\phi^\dagger(\bar\tau;g_\star)\}$ and
$i_\star=\Lambda(g_\star)/\big(1+\Lambda(g_\star)\big)$,*
$$\ln B + \gamma\theta\ln\!\big((1-\alpha)A\big) + \gamma(1-\theta)\ln(\phi_\star\alpha A)
  + \frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln(i_\star\alpha A)
  + \min_{t\in\{\tau_\star,\,\bar\tau\}}\!\Big[\gamma(1-\theta)\ln t
      + \frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln(1-t)\Big] \;>\; \mathcal{H}(g_\star). \tag{E}$$
*Then a stationary profile $(\tau^\ast,\phi^\ast,i^\ast)$ exists, with
$\tau^\ast\in(0,\bar\tau]$, $\phi^\ast\in(0,1]$ and $i^\ast\in(0,1)$,
and it satisfies two of Definition 1’s three clauses: it induces a
unique interior rest point of $(\star)$, locally asymptotically stable
by Theorem 2(iii), and it is admissible there.*

*Proof.* **Step 1: a continuous self-map.** Define
$\hat g = \max\{g_\star,\ \mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{P})\}$ where
$\mathcal{P}$ is defined, and $\hat g = g_\star$ on the three faces
$\tau=0$, $\phi=0$, $i=0$. This is continuous on all of
$[0,\bar\tau]\times[0,1]^{2}$: away from those faces it is a maximum of
two continuous functions, and near any point of them
$\mathcal{P}\to-\infty$ by Proposition 6(ii), so $\hat g\equiv g_\star$
on a neighbourhood. It is bounded,
$\hat g\le\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\sup\mathcal{P})<\infty$, again by
Proposition 6(ii). Since $g_\star>\beta^{1/(\sigma-1)}$, Proposition
6(iii) gives $\tilde\beta\le\beta g_\star^{1-\sigma}<1$ at every point
of the box. The sign content of Propositions 3(i) and 4(iii) and of
Lemma B.2 is algebraic in $(\tilde\beta,g)$ — the wedges are the
quotients $\Omega=\tilde\beta w/D_G$ and $J=\tilde\beta u/D_x$ with
$D_G = g(1-\tilde\beta)+\tilde\beta w$ and
$D_x = g(1-\tilde\beta)+\tilde\beta(1-\alpha)u$, and $\mathcal{M}$ is
(B.7$''$) — so $\Omega\in(0,1)$, $D_x>0$, $J>0$, $\mathcal{M}>0$, $r>0$
and $\Lambda>0$ hold at every point of the box, each continuous in
$\hat g$.

One thing that does *not* hold at every point of the box is the economic
reading. On the region where the truncation binds there is no rest point
at all — that is what Theorem 2(ii) says about the faces — so
$\Gamma_\star$ is there a formal extension of the stationary map and not
a margin evaluated at anything. Step 3 is what makes the extension
harmless: it shows the fixed point never lands in that region, so the
object Brouwer returns is a fixed point of the stationary map itself.

Let $\Gamma_\star$ be (15) with $\hat g$ in place of $g$. Its first
coordinate is continuous because $\Xi$ is jointly continuous and
strictly decreasing in $\tau$ with a transversal crossing, so the root
moves continuously with $(\phi,\hat g)$; its second is continuous
because $\phi^\dagger$ is continuous in $(\tau,\hat g)$ on $\tau>0$ and
exceeds one near $\tau=0$, where the truncation makes the coordinate
identically one; its third is continuous in $\hat g$. And $\Gamma_\star$
maps the box into itself, because
$\min\{\bar\tau,\tau^\dagger\}\in(0,\bar\tau]$,
$\min\{1,\phi^\dagger\}\in(0,1]$ and $\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)\in(0,1)$ — the
truncations do this, and they do it whatever the primitives.

**Step 2: a fixed point.** $[0,\bar\tau]\times[0,1]^{2}$ is compact and
convex and $\Gamma_\star$ is continuous, so Brouwer’s theorem gives
$p^\ast=(\tau^\ast,\phi^\ast,i^\ast)$ with
$\Gamma_\star(p^\ast)=p^\ast$.

**Step 3: the truncation is slack there.** Suppose not, so
$\mathcal{P}(p^\ast)\le\mathcal{H}(g_\star)$ and
$\hat g(p^\ast)=g_\star$. Then $i^\ast=i_\star$;
$\tau^\ast\ge\tau_\star$, because
$\tau^\dagger(\phi;g_\star)\ge\tau^\dagger(1;g_\star)$ by Proposition
7(ii); and $\phi^\ast\ge\phi_\star$, because (7) is strictly decreasing
in $\tau$ and $\tau^\ast\le\bar\tau$. So $p^\ast$ lies in the rectangle
$[\tau_\star,\bar\tau]\times[\phi_\star,1]\times\{i_\star\}$. On that
rectangle $\mathcal{P}$ is bounded below by the left-hand side of (E) —
replace $\kappa_W$ by $1-\alpha$ and $\phi$ by $\phi_\star$, and note
that the remaining terms in $\tau$,
$\gamma(1-\theta)\ln\tau+\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln(1-\tau)$, are
strictly concave and so minimised at an endpoint. Hence
$\mathcal{P}(p^\ast)>\mathcal{H}(g_\star)$ by (E), contradicting the
supposition. Therefore
$\hat g(p^\ast)=\mathcal{H}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{P}(p^\ast)\big)>g_\star$,
and $\Gamma_\star$ agrees with $\Gamma$ at $p^\ast$: the fixed point is
a fixed point of the untruncated map.

**Step 4: what it delivers.** $\tau^\ast>0$, $\phi^\ast>0$ and
$i^\ast\in(0,1)$, and by Step 3 $g>g_\star>\max(1-\delta,1-\delta_G)$,
so the interiority requirement (A) holds at the fixed point rather than
being assumed there. Theorem 2(ii) then applies and gives a unique
interior rest point in $(x,z)$; Theorem 2(i) gives
$\ln\nu^\ast = \Theta^\ast/\gamma$, finite because
$(1-i^\ast)(1-\tau^\ast)>0$, so the class ratio is interior too. By Step
3 and Proposition 6(iii) the profile is proper at that rest point, which
is Assumption 5; and Theorem 2(iii) makes the rest point locally
asymptotically stable. $\blacksquare$

Condition (E) is a finite comparison of explicit expressions in the
primitives, with no fixed point and no simulation inside it: the three
quantities $(\tau_\star,\phi_\star,i_\star)$ are the closed forms (9),
(7) and (12) read at the single growth factor $g_\star$, and the minimum
is over two points. It is also a genuine restriction rather than an
identity, and what it restricts is whether the economy can grow fast
enough to be proper at all: it fails when the reproduction scale or the
instrument ceiling is small enough, and the theorem is silent there.

An existential over an auxiliary number is a weak thing to leave in a
theorem’s hypotheses, and it need not stay there. Condition (E) turns
out to be a floor on a single composite primitive.

**Proposition 8 (condition (E) is a floor on the reproduction scale).**
*Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\theta\in(0,1)$ and $\bar\tau<1$, and
write* $$S \;=\; \ln B \;+\; \frac{\gamma}{1-\alpha}\ln A, \qquad
  g_{\mathrm{fl}} \;=\; \max\big\{1-\delta,\ 1-\delta_G,\ \beta^{1/(\sigma-1)}\big\}.$$
*Then:*

*(i) **$B$ and $A$ enter only through $S$.** Neither appears in
$\Omega$, $J$, $r$, $\Lambda$, $\mathcal{H}$, nor therefore in
$\tau_\star$, $\phi_\star$ or $i_\star$, which are the closed forms (9),
(7) and (12) read at $g_\star$. In $\mathcal{P}$ the three logarithms
carrying $A$ have coefficients summing to
$\gamma\theta+\gamma(1-\theta)+\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)=\gamma/(1-\alpha)$.
Hence (E) at $g_\star$ is exactly $S>\mathcal{E}(g_\star)$, where*
$$\mathcal{E}(g) \;=\; \mathcal{H}(g)\;-\;\gamma\theta\ln(1-\alpha)\;-\;\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(\phi_\star\alpha)
   \;-\;\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln(i_\star\alpha)
   \;-\;\min_{t\in\{\tau_\star,\bar\tau\}}\Big[\gamma(1-\theta)\ln t
        +\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln(1-t)\Big]$$ *carries neither
$B$ nor $A$.*

*(ii) **$\mathcal{E}$ is bounded below.** $\mathcal{E}\to+\infty$ as
$g\to\infty$, because $\tilde\beta\to0$ sends $\Omega$, $J$ and
$\Lambda$ to zero and the vanishing of $\phi_\star$ and $i_\star$ enters
$\mathcal{E}$ with a minus sign. At the floor the behaviour depends on
which constraint binds. Where $g_{\mathrm{fl}}=1-\delta$, the divergence
of $\mathcal{H}$’s capital term is cancelled exactly by $i_\star$, which
is proportional to $\Lambda$ and so to $J$ and so to $g-1+\delta$, both
carrying the coefficient $\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)$; where
$g_{\mathrm{fl}}=\beta^{1/(\sigma-1)}$ every object is finite, $r$ and
$\Lambda$ diverging only through $i_\star\to1$. Where
$g_{\mathrm{fl}}=1-\delta_G$ there is no cancellation and
$\mathcal{E}\to+\infty$: $\mathcal{H}$’s stock term diverges once, while
$\phi_\star$ and $\tau_\star$ are each proportional to $\Omega$ and so
to $g-1+\delta_G$, and each enters with the opposite sign and the same
coefficient $\gamma(1-\theta)$, so the three net to
$-\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(g-1+\delta_G)$.*

*(iii) **The hypothesis is a threshold.** Writing
$S_{\min}=\inf\{\mathcal{E}(g):g>g_{\mathrm{fl}}\}$, which is finite by
(ii), Theorem 3’s hypothesis holds for some admissible $g_\star$ if and
only if $S>S_{\min}$. The infimum need not be attained — at the cells of
Computation 1 it sits at the properness floor, which the theorem
excludes — but $\mathcal{E}$ is continuous and (E) is strict, so any $S$
above $S_{\min}$ supplies an admissible $g_\star$ just above the floor.*
$\blacksquare$

Three of the four terms subtracted in $\mathcal{E}$ are the instruments
the theorem’s own construction pins down, so the threshold is not an
extra object: it is what (E) already was, with the auxiliary number
minimised out. The reading is that Theorem 3 needs the economy to be
productive enough, and how much is enough is now a number rather than a
search.

**Computation 3 (the hypothesis holds at the reported cells).** \*At the
cells of Computation 1 the floor
$\max\{1-\delta,\ 1-\delta_G,\ \beta^{1/(\sigma-1)}\}$ is $0.96$, and
(E) holds at $g_\star=0.97$ with a margin of $0.383$ in units of
$\mathcal{H}$ — and at every one of the eighteen values sampled on a
$0.002$ grid across $[0.9605,\ 0.9945]$. Iterating start $\to$
equilibrium $\to$ induced rest point $\to$ start to a fixed point, the
self-consistent equilibrium at $\omega=0$ is $\tau^\ast=0.50$,
$\phi^\ast=1$ and $i^\ast=0.410$ at $g=1.0103$, reached in six outer
iterations, with $\Omega=0.533$, $J=0.796$, $\rho=4.271$ and
$\Lambda=0.696$. Its rest point is
$(x^\ast,\nu^\ast,z^\ast) = (0.650,\ 0.083,\ 2.375)$, at which the
primitive map $(\star)$ is at rest and $g_W=g_C=g$ to machine precision,
and the private gap (2) holds there at $c_C/c_W = 1.756$. On the
diagonal the closed form (12) and the grid best response differ by
$4\times10^{-4}$ — well inside one grid atom, and the sharpest form of
the start-dependence check Computation 1 reports off it. At
$\omega=0.50$ the same construction gives $(0.51,\ 1,\ 0.61)$ at
$g=1.0252$, with $\Omega=0.519$, $J=0.751$, $\rho=3.104$,
$\Lambda=1.580$, rest point $(0.908,\ 0.058,\ 2.167)$ and
$c_C/c_W=1.620$; at $\omega=0.25$ the outer iteration did not settle
within twenty-five rounds and no self-consistent profile is reported
there.

*Every one of these numbers is what the dynastic paper reports, to the
precision it reports them: $\Omega=0.53$, $J=0.80$, $\Lambda=0.70$, rest
point $(0.652,\ 0.083,\ 2.372)$, wedge $1.756$. The agreement in the
workforce valuation is the one that is not a coincidence and can be
stated as a theorem rather than an observation. Writing
$r_C=\tilde\beta\gamma\rho$, the scalar that enters (12) in the same
slot the dynastic $r$ occupied, its measured value here is $2.029$
against that paper’s $2.03$ — and by the identity of Theorem 6’s
opening, $r_C$ and $r$ satisfy the same relation
$r\mathcal{M}=\tilde\beta(1-\alpha)\gamma(1+\alpha J)$ at $\omega=0$.
They are not two numbers that happen to agree; they are one number
reached from two payoffs.* Minimising $\mathcal{E}$ over the whole of
$(g_{\mathrm{fl}},\infty)$ rather than over that sampled window gives
Proposition 8’s exact thresholds at these cells: $S_{\min}=-0.6987$, so
with $A=1$ condition (E) holds for every $B>0.4972$ and for no $B$ at or
below it, and — by bisection, the ceiling not being additively separable
— for every $\bar\tau \in (0.003366,\ 0.990144)$ and for no $\bar\tau$
outside it. The ceiling’s admissible set is an interval rather than a
half-line, and the upper end is not an artefact of the search range:
$\bar\tau$ enters through a term concave in it and divergent at both
ends, so $S_{\min}\to+\infty$ as $\bar\tau\to1^-$ and (E) fails there
for any fixed scale — a region Assumption 1 admits, since it bounds
$\bar\tau$ only below one. The minimiser sits at the properness floor
$0.96$, outside the window, which is why the window’s own threshold is
the slightly higher $0.5102$; a sampled window can only overstate what
(E) demands. The gap is narrow and the profile that closes it is a
degenerate one — at $B=0.50$ the only admissible $g_\star$ lie within
$10^{-14}$ of the floor, where $\tilde\beta$ is within $10^{-15}$ of one
and $i_\star$ within $10^{-8}$ of the boundary. So $0.50$ is a fair
description of where (E) fails in any usable region, and $0.4972$ is
where it fails exactly.\*

The comparator there is the self-consistent equilibrium and not the
three declared starts of Computation 1, whose $\tau^\ast\in[0.51,0.72]$
is a different object: a constant-share concept is start-dependent, and
what Theorem 3 delivers is the profile whose start is its own rest
point. Against the right comparator the agreement is worth something,
because the two computations share no step. The grid maximises a path
payoff and never writes down a marginal value; $\Gamma$ evaluates three
marginal values and never simulates a path. That they meet is evidence —
at these cells and nowhere else — that neither of the two gaps below is
binding there.

The corner survives the change of object, which is the substantive
reading. Computation 1 reaches $\phi^\ast=1$ by maximising path payoffs
over a grid; Theorem 3 reaches it by a fixed point of the stationary
conditions with no path and no grid; and $\Psi(1)>0$ strictly at the
fixed point, so the corner is where the margin puts it rather than where
the boundary catches it.

**What Theorem 3 does not establish.** A stationary profile satisfies
the first-order content of Definition 1’s third clause, at the rest
point, one instrument at a time. Definition 1 asks for a global best
reply, along the path from a declared start, against the whole set of
constant shares. Two steps separate the two, and both are the ones
Section 2.3 already names. The first is the one-shot bridge: $\Gamma$
evaluates each margin at a fixed balanced-growth factor, while a
deviator moves $g$ too, and that the sign of the one-shot margin gives
the direction of travel of the constant-share best response is measured
at the reported cells and not proved. The second is curvature: a zero of
a margin is a critical point, and turning it into a maximum of the
discounted payoff needs quasi-concavity in the deviating share, which is
nowhere established. Section 8.4 closes the first, and closes the second
for the fiscal split; Section 8.5 reduces it, for the other two
instruments and with the growth factor endogenous, to one explicit
inequality apiece — and Theorem 6, in that same section, then proves the
rate’s, which leaves the tax as the one that remains. That is the count
the abstract reports: two of the three close, the third reduces to an
inequality. What has changed is the shape of what is open. Existence is
no longer blocked by the absence of a candidate, by the geometry of the
instrument box, or by the ceiling on the tax; what blocks it now is the
tax’s unproved inequality between elasticities, the worker’s joint
condition in the pair he owns, and the transfer of either criterion from
the rest point a share induces to a fixed declared start. That is a
narrower obstruction than a regularity statement about the payoff, and
each part of it is stated in the primitives rather than as a hypothesis
about a value function.

## 8.4 The one-shot margin is the total margin

Theorem 4 closes the first step under the existing hypotheses. The
obstacle is not the size of the omitted channels but the absence of
separate expressions for them. Every condition in Section 6 is an
envelope taken at a fixed balanced-growth factor, while a permanent
deviation also moves that factor, the capital against which the
workforce is diluted, and the path of both stocks. Section 6 measures
the first at the reported cells; Proposition 9 supplies separate
expressions for the other two. Written out, the three channels cancel.

Fix constant shares at the interior rest point they induce, and move one
instrument permanently by a small amount. The stocks are predetermined,
so the log-deviations $\hat x_t$ and $\hat z_t$ start at zero and travel
under the reduced map linearised at that rest point. In Assumption 4’s
survival shares $a=(1-\delta)/g$ and $c=(1-\delta_G)/g$ that map is
$$\hat x_{t+1} = a\,\hat x_t + (1-a)\big(\hat\iota + \alpha\hat x_t\big) - \hat\gamma_t, \qquad
  \hat z_{t+1} = c\,\hat z_t + (1-c)\big(\hat\varsigma + \alpha\hat x_t\big) - \hat\gamma_t,$$
$$\hat\gamma_t \;=\; \gamma\theta\big(\hat\kappa + \alpha\hat x_t\big) \;+\; \gamma(1-\theta)\,\hat z_t,$$
where $\hat\gamma_t = \mathrm{d}\ln g_{W,t}$, the coefficients
$\gamma\theta\alpha$ and $\gamma(1-\theta)$ are Proposition 1’s $p$ and
$q$, and the instrument reaches the system through exactly three direct
log-impacts, $$\hat\kappa = \mathrm{d}\ln\kappa_W(\phi), \qquad
  \hat\varsigma = \mathrm{d}\ln(\phi\tau), \qquad
  \hat\iota = \mathrm{d}\ln\big(i(1-\tau)\big),$$ one for the worker’s
consumption share and one for each of the two flows. The $-\hat\gamma_t$
carried by both stock laws is the dilution: the stocks are held per
worker, and the workforce is exactly what the deviation moves.

**Proposition 9 (the two wedges are discounted resolvents).** *Let
Assumptions 1–4 hold and let the profile be proper. Write
$A_x = a+\alpha(1-a)$ for the own-elasticity of capital per worker in
$(\star)$, and*
$$P_x \;=\; \frac{\tilde\beta}{1-\tilde\beta A_x}, \qquad
  P_z \;=\; \frac{\tilde\beta}{1-\tilde\beta c}$$ *for the discounted
resolvents of the two linearised stock laws. Then
$1-\tilde\beta A_x = D_x/g$, both resolvents are strictly positive, and*
$$\Omega \;=\; (1-c)\,P_z, \qquad\qquad J \;=\; (1-a)\,P_x. \tag{16}$$

*Proof.* With $a=(1-\delta)/g$ and $1-a=(g-1+\delta)/g$,
$$1-\tilde\beta A_x \;=\; 1-\tilde\beta a-\tilde\beta\alpha(1-a)
 \;=\; \frac{g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta)-\tilde\beta\alpha(g-1+\delta)}{g} \;=\; \frac{D_x}{g},$$
which is the denominator of $J$ in (8); hence
$(1-a)P_x = \tilde\beta(g-1+\delta)/D_x = J$. For the stock,
$1-\tilde\beta c = \big[g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)\big]/g$ and
$1-c = (g-1+\delta_G)/g$, so
$(1-c)P_z = \tilde\beta(g-1+\delta_G)/\big[g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)\big]$,
which is $\Omega$ in (6). For the signs, $D_x>0$ under properness by
Proposition 4(iii), and
$g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G) = (g-1+\delta_G)+(1-\tilde\beta)(1-\delta_G)>0$
by Assumption 4 together with $\tilde\beta<1$. $\blacksquare$

Appendix B.3$'$ already reads $\Omega$ as a discount factor, times the
share of next period’s stock that one period’s investment buys, times
the discounted survival sum thereafter. Proposition 9 says the same of
$J$, and names what both objects are. They are not analogies for a
transition price. They are the resolvents themselves, which is why they
— and nothing else — survive into the answer below.

**Theorem 4 (the one-shot margin is the total margin).** *Let
Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\theta\in(0,1)$, let the constant-share
profile be admissible at an interior rest point that is locally
asymptotically stable by Theorem 2(iii), and let the declared start be
that rest point. Interiority in the sense of Assumption 4 already puts
the shares off the faces, and (17)–(18) need that: strictly positive
gross investment in the two stocks is $i>0$ and $\phi\tau>0$, hence
$\tau>0$, and an interior $\nu^\ast$ is $i<1$ by Theorem 2(ii). So
$\tau\in(0,1)$, $\phi\in(0,1]$ and $i\in(0,1)$ come with the hypothesis
rather than beside it. Consider a permanent deviation to another
constant share, and let $V_j$ be the deviator’s discounted payoff along
the path $(\star)$ generates — transition, dilution, and the induced
change in the balanced-growth factor all included. Write*
$$\Delta \;=\; 1 \;+\; \gamma\alpha\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]\,P_x \;+\; \gamma(1-\theta)\,P_z.$$
*Then*
$$\frac{\partial V_W}{\partial\phi} = \frac{N_W^{1-\sigma}\,\tilde\beta\,\gamma}
      {(1-\tilde\beta)^{2}\,\Delta}\;\Psi(\phi), \qquad
  \frac{\partial V_W}{\partial\tau} = \frac{N_W^{1-\sigma}\,\tilde\beta\,\gamma\alpha}
      {(1-\tilde\beta)^{2}\,\Delta}\;\Xi(\tau;\phi), \tag{17}$$
$$\frac{\partial V_C}{\partial i} = \frac{P_0^{\,1-\sigma}\,(1-\omega)}
      {1-\tilde\beta}\;\Big[\frac{\Lambda}{i}-\frac{1}{1-i}\Big], \tag{18}$$
*with $\Psi$, $\Xi$ and $\Lambda$ exactly (6), (14) and (12). $\Delta$
is strictly positive and carries no instrument: like $\Omega$ and $J$,
it is a function of $(\tilde\beta,g)$ and the primitives alone. The
capitalist’s prefactor carries no instrument either, and needs no
$\Delta$-analogue at all — under (O) his correction factor is exactly
$1$. Hence every one-shot margin in Propositions 3, 5 and 7 carries the
sign of the corresponding total derivative, and the direction of travel
of the constant-share best response is what those propositions say it
is.*

Display (18) is simpler than the worker’s, and the simplification is the
change of objective speaking. Under a dynastic capitalist the prefactor
was $1+\gamma\tilde\beta/(1-\tilde\beta)$, a dilution correction: his
numbers had to be deflated by the workforce his own accumulation grew.
Under (O) his payoff object scales with the workforce **one-for-one** —
the coefficient on $\ln N_{W}$ in $\ln P$ is exactly one, by the
degree-one homogeneity of Assumption 3 — so there is nothing to correct
and the factor is unity. The one place the objective’s homogeneity is
doing visible work in the body is here.

*Proof.* **Step 1: the payoff derivative is a discounted sum of growth
responses.** Write $W_{j,t}$ for the argument of class $j$’s felicity —
$W_{W,t}=N_{W,t}$ and, under (O), $W_{C,t}=P_t$ — so that each class
maximises $V_j=(1-\sigma)^{-1}\sum_t\beta^tW_{j,t}^{1-\sigma}$. Both
objects are homogeneous of degree one in the state (Proposition A.1),
which is all this step uses. Differentiating termwise — legitimate by
the domination of Appendix A.4, which needs $\tilde\beta<1$ and the
reduced Jacobian’s spectrum strictly inside the unit disc, both in force
under these hypotheses —
$$\frac{\partial V_j}{\partial s} = \sum_{t\ge0}\beta^t W_{j,t}^{1-\sigma}\,
   \frac{\partial \ln W_{j,t}}{\partial s} .$$ For the **worker** the
initial population is predetermined, so
$\partial\ln N_{W,t}/\partial s=\sum_{u<t}\hat\gamma_{W,u}$; on the
incumbent balanced path
$\beta^tN_{W,t}^{1-\sigma} = N_{W,0}^{1-\sigma}\tilde\beta^{t}$, so
exchanging the order of summation,
$$\frac{\partial V_W}{\partial s} \;=\; \frac{N_{W,0}^{1-\sigma}\,\tilde\beta}{1-\tilde\beta}\;S_W,
\qquad S_W \;=\; \sum_{t\ge0}\tilde\beta^{t}\hat\gamma_{W,t}, \tag{19}$$
and since $\sigma>1$ makes $N_{W,0}^{1-\sigma}>0$, the sign of the
derivative is the sign of $S_W$. For the **capitalist** $W_{C,0}=P_0$ is
*not* predetermined — his rate moves his date-zero consumption — so the
date-zero term survives rather than dropping out. That surviving term is
the cost in (10), and Step 4 carries it explicitly instead of absorbing
it into a cumulated sum.

**Step 2: three equations in three sums.** Write
$X=\sum_t\tilde\beta^t\hat x_t$ and $Z=\sum_t\tilde\beta^t\hat z_t$.
Because $\hat x_0=\hat z_0=0$,
$\sum_t\tilde\beta^t\hat x_{t+1}=X/\tilde\beta$ and likewise for $z$, so
the two stock laws and the growth equation read
$$X = P_x\Big[\frac{(1-a)\hat\iota}{1-\tilde\beta}-S\Big], \qquad
  Z = P_z\Big[(1-c)\Big(\frac{\hat\varsigma}{1-\tilde\beta}+\alpha X\Big)-S\Big],$$
$$S = \gamma\theta\Big(\frac{\hat\kappa}{1-\tilde\beta}+\alpha X\Big)+\gamma(1-\theta)Z.$$
Substituting the first two into the third and replacing $(1-c)P_z$ by
$\Omega$ and $(1-a)P_x$ by $J$ throughout — which is (16) — every
resolvent disappears except the two inside $\Delta$, and the system
collapses to one identity,
$$(1-\tilde\beta)\,\Delta\,S \;=\; \gamma\Big[\theta\,\hat\kappa \;+\; (1-\theta)\Omega\,\hat\varsigma
   \;+\; \alpha\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)J\,\hat\iota\Big]. \tag{20}$$

**Step 3: the worker.** The split reaches the consumption share and the
stock flow and leaves the capital flow alone, so
$(\hat\kappa,\hat\varsigma,\hat\iota) = \big(-\tau\alpha/\kappa_W(\phi),\,
1/\phi,\, 0\big)$ and the bracket in (20) is
$(1-\theta)\Omega/\phi - \theta\tau\alpha/\kappa_W(\phi)$, which is
$\Psi(\phi)$. The tax reaches all three, with
$\big((1-\phi)\alpha/\kappa_W(\phi),\, 1/\tau,\, -1/(1-\tau)\big)$, and
the bracket is $\alpha$ times
$\theta(1-\phi)/\kappa_W + (1-\theta)\Omega/(\tau\alpha) - J[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega]/(1-\tau)$,
which is $\alpha\,\Xi(\tau;\phi)$. With (19) these are (17).

**Step 4: the capitalist.** Under (O) his payoff runs on $P$, not on
$N_C$, so the class ratio does not enter and no fourth sum is needed:
Step 2’s three sums are the whole of what he requires. Writing $K=xN_W$
and substituting $Y=AK^{\alpha}N_W^{1-\alpha}$ into
$P=C_C^{1-\omega}K^{\omega}$,
$$\ln P_t \;=\; (1-\omega)\ln\big[(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha A\big] \;+\; \zeta\,\ln x_t \;+\; \ln N_{W,t},$$
the coefficient on $\ln N_{W,t}$ being
$(1-\omega)(1-\alpha)+(1-\omega)\alpha+\omega=1$. Hence, per unit of
$i$,
$\partial\ln P_t/\partial i = -(1-\omega)/(1-i)+\zeta\hat x_t+\hat n_{W,t}$.
On the incumbent balanced path
$\beta^tP_t^{1-\sigma}=P_0^{1-\sigma}\tilde\beta^t$, and
$\sum_t\tilde\beta^t\hat n_{W,t}=\tilde\beta S_W/(1-\tilde\beta)$ by the
exchange of Step 1, so
$$\frac{\partial V_C}{\partial i} \;=\; P_0^{1-\sigma}\Big[
   -\frac{1-\omega}{(1-i)(1-\tilde\beta)} \;+\; \zeta X \;+\; \frac{\tilde\beta\,S_W}{1-\tilde\beta}\Big].$$
The rate reaches the capital flow alone, $\hat\iota=1/i$ with
$\hat\kappa=\hat\varsigma=0$, so (20) gives
$S_W = \gamma\alpha\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]J/\big[i(1-\tilde\beta)\Delta\big]$
and $X = J/\big[i(1-\tilde\beta)\big]-P_xS_W$. Both are proportional to
$1/i$; collecting them against the $1/(1-i)$ term leaves (18), with
$\Lambda$ exactly (12).

That agreement is the load-bearing check of this section. The $\Lambda$
arrived at here is a discounted sum of linearised growth responses; the
$\Lambda$ of (12) is the solution of the $3\times3$ envelope system of
Appendix B.4–B.8. Under a dynastic capitalist the two routes overlapped
— both passed through the class-ratio block, one as a sum and one as an
