of the exercise is that the extension must nest the impossibility
exactly, which it does. Lemma 1 states the relation the other way round,
and that direction is the more useful one. It also forecloses a claim it
is tempting to make: holding the reproduction map common does not escape
the compensating-differential verdict on class-specific technologies.
Expanded in private consumption the worker’s map is class-specific, that
verdict applies, and the wedge is not a condition advantage. What the
verdict leaves open is the case where the class-specific factor is not a
technology at all but a stock the contested instrument accumulates, and
that is the whole of the difference here.

The equalisation itself is not new, and no claim is made on it. In the
dynastic models with endogenous fertility that begin with Barro and
Becker (1989), a parent who can add descendants rather than enrich the
ones he has works the extensive margin, and the transfer to each child
comes out independent of wealth; Alvarez (1999) carries the result into
a stochastic setting, and Córdoba, Liu and Ripoll (2016) put the
consequence plainly, that there is no inequality after the original
generation. The phenomenon is theirs. What differs is the primitive that
produces it. There fertility and transfers are both chosen and
equalisation falls out of a quantity–quality optimisation; here
fertility is not a margin at all but a technology that fuses consumption
and dynasty growth, and the ratio is held by the requirement that two
classes grow at one rate in a stationary state. The two results are
cousins rather than nested. This model does not generalise Barro–Becker,
and what it exhibits is asymptotic and holds only at a stationary $\nu$.

Nearer in setting, and outside that family altogether, is Brezis (2001):
two classes, a proletariat and a business elite, both with endogenous
and class-differentiated fertility, in a model built explicitly on that
class structure. She makes the move against altruistic fertility that
this setup also needs — on her framing children are a necessity for
survival rather than a consumption good — so worker fertility there is
constraint-determined, the minimum number the household’s budget will
carry, and it falls as the wage rises. It is the nearest precedent this
paper has found for treating reproduction as a technology rather than a
preference. It cannot contain the result here, and the reason is a
single assumption: the elite population is taken to be negligible, so
total population is worker population and there is no class ratio left
to have a law of motion. She assumes the size of the non-labouring class
away; here that size is the object.

The public-capital growth literature reached the transition first. Once
a public capital stock accumulates out of tax revenue alongside private
capital the model has two state variables and therefore a transition,
and Futagami, Morita and Shibata (1993) showed in exactly that setting
that the welfare-maximising tax is smaller than the growth-maximising
one. The transition factor $\Omega$ is this paper’s version of that
wedge, and the direction of the over-prediction is theirs. What is this
paper’s own is where the public input sits and what the wedge is asked
to do. The stock enters reproduction, not production, so what it moves
is the class composition and the incidence of reproduction funding
rather than the marginal product of capital. The economy is two-class
rather than representative-agent, which is what allows a fiscal split to
be contested at all, and is why the interesting question here is whether
the capitalist funds the social wage. The two-class setting is not
itself a departure: Tavani and Zamparelli (2016) give the public sector
a productive and redistributive role in a two-class growth model. What
separates this one from theirs is what separates it from Futagami,
Morita and Shibata — there the public stock is a production input and
the model is closed by a growth rule, where here it enters reproduction
and each class holds an instrument it chooses against the other. And
$\Omega$ is below one exactly when the worker’s problem is proper, and
rises with the stock’s depreciation — both facts about this model’s own
structure. That the placement is not a relabelling can be checked rather
than asserted: in a minimal comparison, a stock placed in production has
its output elasticity enter the worker’s own fiscal rule, where a
reproduction map’s outer elasticity provably cannot. Barro (1990) is the
flow antecedent and sits further off: there the public input is an
argument of the production function, and a representative agent has no
class composition for the dilution channel of Section 6 to work through.
A unique and stable transition is in any case not something this paper
discovers: Futagami, Morita and Shibata establish one for their
two-stock model. What differs here is the object — a two-class dynamic
game in discrete time rather than a representative-agent growth model,
with margins that hold identically across the admissible parameter space
rather than at a computed point.

The cross-valuation structure is not this paper’s either. Proposition 5
turns on a marginal value one player places on a state belonging to the
other, and that structure is standard: an asymmetric two-player
differential game generically carries own and cross co-states, and Huato
(2005) already runs a modified Keynes–Ramsey rule in which appropriated
output is valued at the cross shadow price and retained output at the
own one. Grossman and Kim (1996) likewise set a structurally asymmetric
pair of dynasties accumulating against each other. What this model adds
is the object the cross-price is taken on: not a wealth stock that can
be seized but an endogenously reproducing population, which is what lets
a reproduction elasticity enter one class’s accumulation rule while
cancelling from the other’s fiscal rules — a channel that requires both
a population that grows out of consumption and a class that does not own
it.

Huato (2005) studies a two-player production-and-appropriation game in
which intertemporal exchange between the players, and hence any
interest-rate or credit channel, is excluded by assumption. The present
framework is class-structural rather than endowment-symmetric and does
not turn on appropriation, but it is compatible with that programme and,
in the credit-augmented extension the design contemplates, reinstates
precisely the channel Huato sets aside. Huato (2005, p. 29) leaves his
own dynamics open between a “self-correcting mechanism … that prevents
initial disparities from exploding” and nonlinearities that “build up
initial small disparities … to the point of creating conditions that
induce distributional conflict.” Put to this chassis the dichotomy
resolves on the self-correcting side, and the correcting mechanism is
not the concavity of an appropriation function but the endogenous
reproduction rate standing in the denominator of every transition. His
is a continuous-time appropriation game and this a discrete-time fiscal
one, so what is settled is the dichotomy on this chassis, not his
conjecture.

# 10. Conclusion

The equalisation boundary is arithmetic, and this paper does not dispute
it. A stationary class ratio means equal dynasty growth; a common,
strictly increasing reproduction map turns equal growth into equal
reproduction; and no instrument appears anywhere in that chain. The
question asked here was whether granting the boundary leaves the
contested instruments inert, as it does at the no-social-wage corner,
where the private gap is identically one whatever the policy.

It does not. Adding one object the boundary’s setup excludes — public
reproduction infrastructure, funded by the tax that is already the
worker’s instrument, entering the worker’s reproduction and not the
capitalist’s — leaves the boundary binding on the reproduction metric
while giving the instruments a distributional object they could not
previously touch. Lemma 1 locates why: what a stationary ratio equalises
is a reproduction index, and the no-social-wage corner is where that
index is private consumption itself. Where the index is a composite, the
private per-capita gap is a closed form in the stationary quantities,
policy-dependent through the public stock, with a constant negative
elasticity along the stationarity locus (Theorem 1). That is the
Speenhamland shape — a public supplement co-moving against the private
claim — with its welfare sign left open, because the social wage here is
capital-funded. Which class holds the larger private claim is a
different question from that displacement. Theorem 1 orders the two
claims by the public stock alone, and the ordering and the displacement
are independent: each holds under composites where the other fails.

A second margin sits alongside that one, and it is not a per-capita
object. At a stationary ratio Lemma 1 equalises the class *condition*;
the relative *size* of the two classes it leaves alone. Theorem 2(i)
makes $\ln\nu$ a contraction whose $\Theta$ is free of $\nu$, so the
stationary ratio is $\ln\nu^\ast=\Theta^\ast/\gamma$ — a closed form in
the instruments and the primitives. Theorem 2$'$ makes that form
explicit, and the capital stock cancels out of it, leaving the
instruments and the growth factor alone; the no-social-wage ratio
Proposition 2(ii) states on the unfunded face turns out to hold across
the whole split range once the worker’s index is private consumption. At
that corner the growth factor drops out too, and with it the whole
technology: relative class size there is a function of the three
instruments and the capital share and of nothing else — not
productivity, not either depreciation rate, and no feature of the
reproduction technology at all, its functional form included, since that
half of the argument is Lemma 1’s and Lemma 1 holds for an arbitrary
common increasing map. All three instruments are signed there. Public
provision destroys both properties at once, returning the technology to
the ratio and reversing signs rather than shading them. Read off it,
reinvestment lowers the capitalist class’s relative size at every rate
where the worker reproduces on private consumption alone, and the
public-input weight is the only channel that can reverse the sign — so
the wedge that separates reproduction from private consumption in
Theorem 1 is the wedge that unsettles the direction here. What equal
reproduction forecloses is a difference in condition, not a difference
in number.

That yields the model’s sharpest statement about what fixes those
conditions, and it is a result here rather than a premise. What carries
it is not the reproduction technology: that is one strictly increasing
map, common to both classes, exactly as Assumption 2 states it. What
carries it is the composite the map is applied to — one class can reach
that composite through the public stock and the other cannot. On that
margin, and by that route, the conditions on which class continuation
rests are not fixed by biology or by preference: they are a contested
outcome, built by instruments the two classes set against each other.
Proposition 2(iii) shows how little it takes to end the worker’s line —
the worker’s own split at zero, with the stock fully depreciating, and
no move by the capitalist required.

The dependence is mutual. The public stock ties the worker’s
reproduction to capital income, and the capitalist’s own interior
reinvestment rate rises with the price he puts on the workforce he must
reproduce, the rest of his rate rule held fixed — a closed form in the
primitives, entering his rate directly, and arising because labour is an
input his production needs rather than because he values worker numbers
as such. That the dependence is a partial and not a comparative static
in the primitives is the paper’s own finding: what the rule contains is
the price weighted by the reproduction elasticity, and Section 6 reports
a sweep along which the price falls while the rate rises.

That the social wage is capital-funded is the model’s structure and not
a calibration, and Proposition 2(iii) is what gives it teeth. The public
stock’s law of motion has one source term, a fraction of the capital
share; the wage bill funds none of it. Where $\theta<1$ the stock so
funded is not an amenity. Unfunded, the worker’s dynasty contracts at
exactly the stock’s depreciation rate, and it does so at every tax rate;
where the stock fully depreciates, the dynasty is extinguished in one
step and the payoff is $-\infty$. So the instrument that reaches the
stock on which worker reproduction depends is funded out of the income
of the class the worker contests. Within this instrument set the two are
not separable. At the split’s corner his private claim is exactly the
competitive wage bill, and the whole of his redistributive tax has
become public provision with none of it private income. Nor is the
constraint imposed on him from outside. Proposition 4(iii)’s closed form
puts the entire gap between his optimal tax and full expropriation in
the capital wedge — the public wedge read on the other stock — and the
instrument ceiling $\bar\tau$ does no work at the reported cells. The
scope of that reading is the model’s instrument set: one tax base, one
route to the stock. That an economy affords no other route is not shown
here and is not asserted, and neither is the corner itself a general
fact — Proposition 3(iii) gives it under an explicit inequality between
the tax and the transition wedge, and the sweep returns it at nine of
the ten distinct proper cells and not at the tenth.

Within the constant-share class, the stable long-run configuration is
not a numerical accident. The class ratio follows a first-order process,
the interior rest point is unique, and the local stability bound holds
across the admissible parameter space. The same outer elasticity that
keeps this long-run state stable also supports the fiscal asymmetry. An
interior stationary profile exists under an explicit primitive
condition, with each instrument satisfying its own stationary decision
rule. A second uniqueness sits above that one and is a different object:
not the rest point in the state, but the balanced-growth factor the
profile composes to. Where the two transition wedges are jointly signed
it is unique as well — under one further sign condition in general, and
under the wedge condition alone on an explicit band of the capital
share, the public-input weight and the curvature. Where that holds, the
start-dependence the constant-share class carries is a dependence in the
shares and not in the rate they generate.

The reported equilibrium levels, fiscal corner, rule comparisons, and
leader-follower exercises are computations. They establish what holds at
their reported cells and rule families, not a general financing
relation. What they establish there is worth stating rather than only
bounding: the state-contingent deviation that pays against a fixed
opponent is extinguished by the opponent’s reply, while the widenings
that do beat the constant-share equilibrium require commitment, are not
the deviating class’s own best commitment, and are not equilibria of the
game. The paper’s formal results are stronger at the stationary level
than the computations, but they do not certify a general state-dependent
equilibrium or exclude cycles far from the long-run state.

A stationary profile is not yet a mutual best reply from an arbitrary
declared start. At the rest point it induces, the stationary decision
margins are the full-payoff derivatives and the fiscal split and rate
each satisfy a single-crossing criterion. Moving a deviation off that
rest point requires curvature along the transition path. For the
capitalist, the remaining fixed-start curvature condition holds at
reported cells and fails elsewhere inside the assumptions. For the
worker, the tax condition likewise holds at reported cells and fails
elsewhere inside the assumptions; separate single-crossing in tax and
split does not rule out a saddle, so the joint condition closes when
both stocks fully depreciate and remains open otherwise. The durability
of the funded stocks is the residual formal obstacle. The capitalist’s
valuation of the workforce, by contrast, is signed by two independent
derivations.

The capitalist’s objective makes this a model of contested accumulation
rather than a symmetric dynastic game. Valuing both retained consumption
and owned capital preserves a trade-off. Valuing capital alone removes
it: reinvestment has no cost in the objective and moves to its corner.
The interior objective family is therefore not a calibration choice but
the domain on which accumulation remains a decision.

The natural extensions are organised in Section 11: a broader
reproduction composite, multiple funding sources, intertemporal
exchange, and public influence over accumulation all change the objects
whose incidence the present model isolates.

What the argument is for is a distinction that ordinary distributional
comparisons can miss. A stationary class ratio can coexist with a
private-consumption gap because public provision enters the worker’s
reproduction input. The gap is neither evidence that the worker is
better off nor evidence that the capitalist is worse off: those are
welfare questions outside the model. What is established is narrower and
sharper. When the public stock matters for worker reproduction, the
source of its finance and the private claim it displaces cannot be read
as independent policy objects.

# 11. Future Research: Financing, Provision, and Accumulation

Two stylized facts give the next theory its direction. First, public
social protection combines cash and in-kind benefits; in 2023, cash
benefits were below 60% of social-protection benefits in Finland,
Denmark, and Sweden (Eurostat 2025). Second, China combines improved
access to services with a rebalancing process toward consumption that
remains gradual (World Bank 2026). Neither fact makes either setting an
instance of this game. Together they identify the missing theoretical
margins.

The multi-source finance extension is a Nordic-motivated theory problem.
Replace the single capital-income tax base with several revenue sources
and allow the public stock to coexist with a cash transfer. The question
is whether the private-consumption gap survives when the public stock is
financed partly from labour income, consumption taxes, or social
contributions, and whether the incidence theorem can identify the
contribution of each source separately. The present model provides the
benchmark precisely because it holds those margins fixed.

The public-private accumulation extension is a China-motivated theory
problem. To represent a mixed setting in which public authority affects
both provision and accumulation, the successor model must add a public
or state-directed capital margin rather than treating reinvestment as
wholly private. The question then is not whether the present result
applies to China. It is whether public influence over accumulation
changes the separation between the public stock that sustains
reproduction and the private claim it displaces.

Two further extensions already follow from the present derivations. The
fiscal asymmetry survives an arbitrary increasing outer reproduction map
and an arbitrary composite; a successor should therefore replace
Cobb–Douglas only to study how state-dependent input shares alter the
closed form, not to test whether the mechanism survives. And an
intertemporal-exchange extension should introduce credit or an
interest-rate margin explicitly, because a claim on accumulated capital
changes the worker’s fiscal choice rather than merely adding a financial
label to it.

Both extensions should preserve the paper’s central discipline. The
relevant objects must be kept separate: in-kind provision, private
household claims, funding sources, and accumulation responses. Neither
aggregate public spending nor household consumption alone identifies
their incidence. The task for future theory is to derive the conditions
under which those margins collapse into one another, or remain distinct.

reduction, the complete derivation of the envelope conditions with the
two sign lemmas, the computational procedure with its grids, horizons
and admissibility masks, the full sweep tables, the state-contingent
rule censuses behind Computation 2, the robustness comparisons, and the
replication statement.*
